# Hayles, the thirteen volumes, 1984 to 2025: one file for the deep read

Started 30 Oct 2026. **This is the single file for the whole Hayles deep read.** Part 1 is the spine: the named concepts across the thirteen volumes, one row per concept, one column per book, with page numbers, quotations and a citation form in every filled cell. Part 2 onward is one part per volume, in chronological order (Part 2: 1984; Part 3: 1990; Part 4: 1991, introduction only; Part 5: 1999; Part 6: 2002; Part 7: 2004, introduction only; Part 8: 2005; Part 9: 2008; Part 10: 2012; Part 11: 2013, introduction with Pressman and section forewords only; Part 12: 2017; Part 13: 2021; Part 14: 2025), each a close read on the pattern of `HAYLES_CosmicWeb_ch1_ch3_closeread.md`. Appendices hold the tables too long for the body. Later volumes are added to this file rather than to new files; each addition is dated in its own heading, and the spine cells it fills are dated in the notes at the foot.

The brief is `K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\HANDOFF_Hayles_deep_read_2026-10-30_v3.md`. What it settles (sections 3, 9 and 10) is not re-argued here.

**Standing conventions in this file.** Page numbers are printed folios. Every quotation names its page. "Cite:" gives the form to paste, keyed to the reference list in 1.3. Counts give their denominator. A cell reading "not yet read" means the book has not been opened for that row and nothing may be inferred from it.

---

## Part 1. The spine

Books, in the order of the columns: **1984** *The Cosmic Web* (CW); **1990** *Chaos Bound* (CB); **1991** *Chaos and Order* (ed.); **1999** *How We Became Posthuman* (HWBP); **2002** *Writing Machines* (WM); **2004** *Nanoculture* (ed.); **2005** *My Mother Was a Computer* (MMWC); **2008** *Electronic Literature* (EL); **2012** *How We Think* (HWT); **2013** *Comparative Textual Media* (ed., with Pressman); **2017** *Unthought*; **2021** *Postprint*; **2025** *Bacteria to AI*.

The 1984 column is read from `K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\9781501722974.pdf` (the Cornell file), in which the PDF index equals the printed page. Its detail is in `K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\HAYLES_CosmicWeb_deepread_Cornell_2026-10-30.md`. Counts in the 1984 column are whole-word matches over the running text of pp. 9-197 (68,122 words), with footnotes (5,698 words) counted separately where stated. Counts in the 1990 column are whole-word matches over pp. 1-295 of *Chaos Bound* (111,966 words, footnotes included). Counts in the 1991 column are over her Introduction to *Chaos and Order*, pp. 1-33 (14,128 words, notes and works cited included); where a contributor's page is given it comes from a term scan of the whole volume (124,204 words) and not from a reading. Counts in the 1999 column are whole-word matches over pp. 1-291 of *How We Became Posthuman* (126,745 words, PDF indices 15-305, notes excluded). Counts in the 2002 column are whole-word matches over the text layer of *Writing Machines*, PDF indices 4-144 (33,504 words); the design sets some running text and all quoted samples as images, so these counts are floors and the pages affected are listed in Part 6. Counts in the 2004 column are over her Introduction to *Nanoculture*, pp. 11-23 (7,658 words of text layer, italic titles unmapped); where a contributor's page is given it comes from a term scan of the whole volume (86,242 words) and not from a reading. Counts in the 2005 column are whole-word matches over pp. 1-243 of *My Mother Was a Computer* (106,910 words, PDF indices 10-252, notes excluded). Counts in the 2008 column are whole-word matches over pp. 1-186 of *Electronic Literature* (50,910 words, PDF indices 15-200, notes excluded; the native text layer carries the "fi" ligature, so words containing it were counted on a normalised pass). Counts in the 2012 column are whole-word matches over pp. 1-248 of *How We Think* (90,495 words, PDF indices 15-262, notes and Works Cited excluded; native text layer). Counts in the 2013 column are whole-word matches over the Introduction only, pp. vii-xxxiii of *Comparative Textual Media* (9,790 words, PDF indices 7-33, reference list included); the three section forewords (pp. 1-3, 97-99, 199-200, about 2,000 words) were read but not counted; the Introduction is co-written with Jessica Pressman and every 2013 cell says so. Counts in the 2017 column are whole-word matches over pp. 1-216 of *Unthought* (79,739 words, PDF indices 13-228, notes excluded) after the small-capital running heads were stripped from every page; an unstripped pass inflates "cognitive nonconscious" and "cognitive assemblage" by about one per page because the Part 1 and Part 2 heads carry those phrases. Counts in the 2021 column are whole-word matches over pp. 1-190 of *Postprint* (58,158 words, PDF indices 15-204, notes excluded) after the running heads were stripped; words containing the "ff", "fi" and "fl" ligatures, which the text layer splits with a space, were counted on a normalised pass and are marked so in Part 13. Counts in the 2025 column are whole-word matches over pp. 1-248 of *Bacteria to AI* (109,294 words, PDF indices 7-254, notes and bibliography excluded) after the letter-spaced running heads and folios were stripped and soft hyphens removed.

The concepts in the first fifteen rows are the starting list from the handoff brief, section 5. Rows 16 to 24 were added by the 1984 reading, rows 25 to 30 by the 1990 reading; the 1991 reading added none; rows 31 to 35 were added by the 1999 reading, row 36 by the 2002 reading and rows 37 and 38 by the 2005 reading; the 2004 and 2008 readings added none; rows 39 and 40 were added by the 2012 reading; the 2013 reading added none; rows 41 and 42 were added by the 2017 reading; rows 43 and 44 were added by the 2021 reading; rows 45 to 47 were added by the 2025 reading; their earlier-year cells were filled from the readings already made, marked "nearest" where the concept is present under another name. For the three edited volumes a cell is filled from Hayles's own introduction only, and says so; where a contributor's uptake of her term is recorded (1991, 2004) the cell names the contributor. Later books will add rows rather than be forced into these.

## Part 2. 1984, *The Cosmic Web*, deep read against the Cornell file

N. Katherine Hayles, *The Cosmic Web: Scientific Field Models and Literary Strategies in the Twentieth Century*. Ithaca and London: Cornell University Press, 1984. From the copyright page of the held file: "Open access edition funded by the National Endowment for the Humanities/Andrew W. Mellon Foundation Humanities Open Book Program"; ISBN-13 978-0-8014-1742-9 (cloth), 978-0-8014-9290-7 (pbk.); text licensed CC BY-NC-ND 4.0. Preface signed "Rolla, Missouri". Chapters 3, 4 and 6 appeared earlier as articles in *Mosaic* (September 1982), *Contemporary Literature* (Winter 1982) and *The Markham Review* (Summer 1983) (p. 12).

`K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\9781501722974.pdf`

Read 30 Oct 2026, the whole book: Preface pp. 9-12, Introduction pp. 15-28, chapter 1 pp. 31-59, chapters 2 to 6 pp. 63-197, References Cited pp. 199-204, Index pp. 205-208. 

**The two earlier close reads stand and are not repeated here.** `HAYLES_CosmicWeb_ch1_ch3_closeread.md` and `HAYLES_CosmicWeb_ch2_ch4_ch5_ch6_closeread.md` (both 29 Oct 2026) read the chapters; section 2.2 below records what happened when their quotations were walked through the Cornell file. This file does what those two did not: it reads the Preface, tracks the named concepts across the whole book with page numbers for the spine, and states the term counts with their denominators. The spine is Part 1 of this file.

**Folio rule for this file.** In the Cornell file the PDF page index equals the printed page number from p. 7 (Contents) to p. 208 (end of Index). Checked on rendered pages at 7, 21, 22, 23, 53, 54, 59, 95, 110, 136, 168, 181, 185 and 202, and in the text layer of every page from 9 to 208. Every page number below is a printed folio and can be opened directly at that PDF index. Any PDF index recorded in a file dated before 30 Oct 2026 refers to the superseded copy and is one too low for this file.

#### 2.2.3 The named concepts in 1984, page by page

The order follows the spine. Each entry gives what the book says, in its words, with the page, and the whole-word count over pp. 9-197 (denominator 68,122 words of running text).

#### 2.3.1 The field concept

The Preface defines it, and the Preface was not read before today.

> "In this book I have singled out the 'field concept' as the theme that is at the heart of this revolution ... **The field concept, as I use the term, is not identical with any single field formulation in science.** For the men and women who work with the various scientific field models and theories from day to day, they have highly specific meanings and applications. The term 'field concept,' by contrast, **draws from many different models those features that are isomorphic**, and hence that are characteristic of twentieth-century thought in general." (p. 9)

> "Perhaps most essential to the field concept is the notion that **things are interconnected** ... a field view of reality pictures objects, events, and observer as belonging inextricably to the same field; the disposition of each, in this view, is influenced, sometimes dramatically, sometimes subtly, but in every instance, by the disposition of the others." (pp. 9-10)

> "Another aspect of the field concept ... is the notion of the **self-referentiality of language**. Because everything, in the field view, is connected to everything else by means of the mediating field, the autonomy assigned to individual events by language is illusory." (p. 10)

The Introduction gives it its physics: "What the particle was for the Newtonian paradigm, the field is for the new paradigm" (p. 16); "Its distinguishing characteristics, then, are its fluid, dynamic nature, the inclusion of the observer, the absence of detachable parts, and the mutuality of component interactions" (p. 15). Counts: "field concept" 81, "field model(s)" 25, "field view" 25, "field" in any use 249. Index: "Field concept, 19, 32, 42, 55; definition of, 9-10, 15-17; Einstein and, 48-49; epistemological implications of, 27-28; influence on writers, 24-25; language and, 41-42; limits to classical analysis, 31-36; resistance to articulation, 19-21, 55, 59".

#### 2.3.2 The field theory of culture, the societal matrix, the cultural matrix

Settled in `HAYLES_field_theory_of_culture_uptake_2026-10-30.md` and not re-argued. Three additions from reading the whole book:

- **The theory is "assumed", in her word, p. 24**: "My purpose in sketching these possibilities obviously is not to write this history, but to show that it is possible ... Rather than attempt this history, **I have assumed it** by locating a group of representative novels within a larger cultural context that includes physics, mathematics, and philosophy." That is the book's own description of its method with respect to the field theory of culture.
- **"Cultural matrix" is used four times, not three**: pp. 23 (twice), 26, and the footnote at p. 66 n. 4 (the Pirsig case, already recorded in the ch2 read). The index entry is "Cultural matrix, 23-24", which omits p. 26. "Societal matrix" once, p. 22. "Climate of opinion" twice, both p. 22. Whitehead once in running text, p. 22, with no note.
- **Who participates in the matrix, p. 26**: Lawrence and Nabokov "demonstrate how writers who are relatively ignorant of the new science nevertheless **participate in the cultural matrix** and so, willy-nilly, encounter in some form the matrix's underlying paradigm."

#### 2.3.3 Causation refused; "both affect"

The Preface states the anti-causal position before the Introduction's p. 22 does, and it is the one place in the book where the word "affect" carries the mutuality:

> "One of the many ideas that the field view revises is the notion of a one-way chain of reaction between the event labeled as the 'cause' and that labeled the 'effect.' Although I spoke earlier of the 'influence' of the field view on modern literature, **I do not mean to imply by this that the literature I discuss is 'caused' by scientific field models.** Rather, the literature is an imaginative response to complexities and ambiguities that are implicit in the models but that are often not explicitly recognized. Thus a comprehensive picture of the field concept is more likely to emerge from the literature and from science viewed together than from either one alone. In this sense **the literature is as much an influence on the scientific models as the models are on the literature, for both affect our understanding of what the field concept means in its totality.**" (p. 10; "affect" is OCR'd "afkct" in the text layer, read from the render of the page)

And at pp. 24-25, on how the influence reaches writers who know no science: "With few exceptions, these authors are reacting not to science as such, but to a more general set of ideas pervasive in the culture."

**Affect, counted.** The open item in the brief (section 8) asks whether affect ever does work in Hayles. In 1984: "affect" as a verb, 9 occurrences (affects 1 at p. 18; affecting 3 at pp. 15, 17, 28; affected 4 at pp. 24, 25, 25, 56; and the p. 10 instance above). **The noun "affect": 0. "Affective": 0.** "Emotion*" 11 (pp. 66, 68, 74, 85, 88, 97, 108, 109, 177), "feeling(s)" 11. Every hit was read in place; "affection(s)" (pp. 78, 132) is excluded. So in the first book the word is a verb for influence and never a name for a register of experience.

#### 2.3.4 The problem of articulation

Named once, at the close of chapter 1: "What our survey of the field concept in various scientific models has shown is that **the problem of articulation is intrinsic to this view of reality**, whether the language involved is the binary sequence of computer programs, the 'wave-packet' equations of quantum mechanics, or one of the syntactically linear natural languages" (p. 59). Built at p. 21 (the web is "articulated, in the double sense of being utterances and of being composed of discrete units joined together"), p. 55 ("the extreme difficulty of translating this intuitive vision into an articulated model"), p. 58 ("What remains unclear is whether it can ever be adequately expressed in an articulated model"). "articulat*" 21 in running text. The question form at pp. 25-26 and 27 is already in the ch1_ch3 read, section 4.

#### 2.3.5 Self-referentiality

From the Preface (p. 10, above) through the thesis sentence of chapter 1: "As self-referentiality of language is virtually the defining characteristic of post-modern criticism and texts, so is it also of post-Newtonian science. Whether the topic under discussion is Gödel's theorem or *Gravity's Rainbow*, self-referentiality is a crucial issue" (p. 41). The mechanism, p. 41: "When language is part of the mediating field ... it participates in the interconnection at the same time that it purports to describe it. To admit the field concept thus entails admitting that the self-referentiality of language is not accidental, but an essential consequence of speaking from within the field." "self-referen*" 37; "Strange Loop" 16 (pp. 34, 36, 142-161). Index: "Self-referentiality, 10, 32; field concept and, 40-42; positivist program and, 36-40; post-modern fiction and, 36-37; post-Newtonian science and, 41-42".

#### 2.3.6 The observer inside the field

p. 10 (above); pp. 19-20, the kaleidoscope ("there will always be one place we can never see at all, the spot we are standing on"); p. 21 ("What is captured by the cosmic web is thus not the elusive whole, but the observer who would speak that whole"); p. 31 ("the Cartesian dichotomy between the *res cogitans* and the *res extensa* ... is not absolute, but an arbitrary product of the human mind"; "Classical physics assumed that it was possible to make a rigorous separation between the observer and what she or he observes"); p. 49 ("there is no such thing as observing this interactive whole from a frame of reference removed from it ... Necessarily and irrevocably we are within it, part of the cosmic web"); p. 51 (Heisenberg: "there is no way to measure a system without interacting with it, and no way to interact with it without disturbing it"). "observer(s)" 28.

#### 2.3.7 Bohr and complementarity: the whole inventory

This is the live item for the proposal (brief, section 8), so every appearance is listed.

| p. | What Hayles says |
|---|---|
| 48 | "With quantum mechanics, especially as interpreted by Niels Bohr, this expansion [of the field concept to include the language of observation] took place within physics itself." |
| 51 | "Under the influence of Niels Bohr, Heisenberg gradually came to the view that the process of measurement in some way determines the values, brings into actuality what was before a potentiality." Heisenberg quoted: "what we observe is not nature in itself but nature exposed to our method of questioning" (n. 35, *Physics and Philosophy* p. 58). |
| 52 | Bohr against Heisenberg: "Bohr never endorsed the 'disturbance' concept of Heisenberg. Rather, in his view, the Uncertainty Relation was deeply bound up with the limitations of language." Petersen: Bohr "loved to repeat that '**we are suspended in language**'" (n. 37, *Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists* 19, Sept. 1963, p. 10). "It is in his philosophy that the connecting links between a field view of language and the field concepts of quantum mechanics are clearest." The quantum of action "an area within which no further distinction between the observer and system is possible." Bohr quoted (n. 38, via Hooker p. 138): "forces us to adopt a new mode of description designated as **complementary** in the sense that any given application of classical concepts precludes the simultaneous use of other classical concepts which in a different connection are equally necessary for the elucidation of the phenomena." |
| 53 | "The classical concepts, Bohr felt, evolved as a consequence of our experience in the world; they reflect the essential distinction between subject and object that is the absolute prerequisite for the process of observation to begin." Bohr quoted (n. 39, Hooker p. 141): "follows ... the meaning of every concept, or rather every word, the meaning depending upon our arbitrary choice of viewpoint." Then the viewpoints argument in Hayles's own words: "To speak is to speak from a position that is defined as separate and distinct from that which is spoken about. Language thus implies a viewpoint, a specific place at which the subject-object split is made ... **But these viewpoints will be mutually exclusive, because the subject-object split can only be made in one place at a time.**" Bohr quoted (n. 40): "a complete elucidation of one and the same object may require diverse points of view which defy a unique description." |
| 53-54 | Hayles recapitulates Bohr "through a consideration of the deep structure of Indo-European languages": actee and actant, verb tenses; "to speak is to use a linguistic structure built on such distinctions." |
| 54 | "For Bohr, the fact that we remain 'suspended in language' does not mean that we cannot make progress; he would therefore be unwilling to subscribe to Pynchon's fatalistic view." Bohr quoted (n. 41, Hooker p. 139) on concepts "based on assumptions initially unnoticed" and "unrecognized presuppositions". |
| 54-55 | "**Thus progress is made not by ignoring or underplaying limitations of viewpoint, but by systematically examining and exploiting them.** It would be possible to write the history of the modern novel using similar terms, starting from a Jamesian theory of point of view and progressing to post-modern literature in which the assumption that there is a 'point' from which to 'view' is called into question. **Shifting viewpoints that are mutually exclusive but all in some sense true**; experiments that involve making the subject-object split in different places; the radical questioning of what it means to be 'objective': these are familiar to literary readers as the central issues of modernism." |
| 57 | Jammer: "Bohr's complementarity interpretation seemed to be less bizarre" than non-local hidden variables. |
| 65 | Pirsig's Quality as "the moment when subject and object meet ... **The reader will recognize in this formulation a model very similar to the one Bohr proposed** in his interpretation of the Uncertainty Principle." |
| 87 | Keller: the Copenhagen Interpretation "an umbrella term 'under which a host of different, often contradictory positions co-reside'"; "Bohr and Heisenberg, though taking very different positions on the Uncertainty Relation, are nevertheless perceived by the scientific community as being of one mind on the matter. Even Heisenberg thought they were in agreement (though Bohr knew better)." |
| 95 | Lawrence's paradox "is a dilemma that Bohr also recognized as fundamental, for to speak is to enter into the 'either-or' conceptualizations that quantum theory, like Lawrence, was trying to escape." |
| 97 | "**Bohr, for example, repeatedly emphasized that science is not about reality, but about what we can say about reality.** For Lawrence, almost the opposite is true." |

Counts: "Bohr" 32 in running text, 3 in footnotes; "Uncertainty Relation/Principle" 18; "viewpoint(s)" 20, of which 13 on pp. 53-54; "complementar*" 5, **two in Bohr's sense (pp. 52, 57) and three in the ordinary sense** ("their basic complementarity", Birkin and Ursula, p. 100; Terra and Antiterra "parallel and complementary", p. 126; "a complementary and completing half of the arc", p. 129). The word "complementarity" as a named principle is Jammer's at p. 57; Hayles's own term for it throughout is "viewpoint". Index: "Bohr, Niels, 48, 54, 65, 97; interpretation of Uncertainty Relation, 51-55; quantum of action, 52-53; theory of language, 52-54".

**One attribution to watch.** The aphorism that science is about what can be said about nature is given to Bohr at p. 97 (above) and to Heisenberg at p. 84: "What Pirsig knows, but cannot fully accept, is that **(as Heisenberg said of science)** literature is not about reality but about what we can say about reality." Neither sentence carries a note. The Heisenberg sentence Hayles does cite, at p. 51 n. 35, is "what we observe is not nature in itself but nature exposed to our method of questioning" (*Physics and Philosophy* p. 58), which is a different sentence. The Bohr version is the one usually quoted, from Petersen 1963, whom she cites at p. 52 n. 37 for "suspended in language". Before either page is cited for the aphorism, Petersen pp. 10-11 should be read; this is a chase item (section 6).

#### 2.3.8 Isomorphism

The book's method word, four times: p. 9 (the field concept "draws from many different models those features that are isomorphic"); p. 41 ("What has not been sufficiently recognized by either is the isomorphism of the two propositions"); p. 55 ("If the isomorphism between the scientific models and literary strategies holds, these limitations will have something to tell us about related limitations in the literary strategies"); p. 175 (paranoid, acid, visionary ways of organising experience "are isomorphic in the sense that they all presuppose the pervasiveness of pattern, that is to say, they all suppose a field view of reality"). The concept-sweep CSV of 30 Oct already notes that Hayles uses isomorphism "of models not cultures"; p. 175 is the one place it is used of modes of experience.

#### 2.3.9 Cognition, consciousness, and the one sentence about species

"cognit*" 41 in running text, 32 of them in the Pynchon chapter, where cognition is the problem rather than the instrument:

- p. 27: Pynchon renders "not at all the simultaneous interactions of a field concept, but fallen, preterite versions of it may be all our **cognitive consciousness** can grasp."
- p. 169: "the focus for the text's anxiety is precisely the cognitive thought that seeks to organize diverse data into coherent patterns. The source of the tension, in other words, lies in the nature of human consciousness itself." And: "contemporary philosophers of science and linguists have suggested that the act of cognition is not merely a passive observation of a world 'out there,' but the active creation of a world that is then perceived as separate from the cognitive faculties that brought it into being."
- p. 173: the solution "implies abandoning our very identity as *homo sapiens*, the creature who knows ... **Cognition is to be replaced by an appreciation for the synchronicity of Nature in which humans do not stand outside as originators of pattern, but take their place within already existing harmonies that include and subsume them.**"
- p. 174 and n. 8: cognition as the subordination of detail into background, with Neisser's "constructive role of cognition" ("Cognition is the sum of those processes that create from the incoming sensory data a recognizable picture of the world").
- p. 179: "To be able to decode the text is to be cognitively conscious, and to be cognitively conscious is to deny the message implicit in that decoding."
- p. 188: "To stay at the barricades, however, is to remain in the realm of cognitive thought."

The species sentence is at **p. 54**, in the Bohr section, not the Pynchon chapter: "As Pynchon explores the dependence of cognition on breaking a unified field into separate and isolated components, he mourns for the holistic, nonfragmented reality that he imagines **other species can sense**." "species" occurs once in the book. "nonconscious" 0; "unconscious" 42 (ch. 3's Lawrence term); "consciousness" 33.

#### 2.3.10 Embodiment and the body

"embod*" 12 times, and **not once in the sense the later books give it**. Every instance is a concept, assumption or quality instantiated in something: "phenomena that embody it" (p. 9), "embodiments of the field concept" (p. 10), the deep structure of Indo-European languages "embodies its fundamental assumptions" (p. 16), "a suitable rhetorical mode in which to embody Quality" (p. 67), "embodies the very duality" (p. 69), "a dramatic embodiment of Quality" (p. 82), "a literature that attempts to embody this view" (p. 83), "the symmetries he wishes them to embody" (p. 122), Nabokov's fiction "embodied a tension" (p. 126), "embodied in two forms" (p. 170), "embody and affirm it" (p. 172), words that "purport to embody or reflect that endless streaming" (p. 185).

The body as a subject is the Lawrence chapter only: "symmetrically arranged 'centers' in the body" (p. 85); "the body centers of one person engage those of another" (p. 86); the plexus and ganglion scheme (pp. 104-105), with "two great tensions" incarnate "in the human body" (p. 104) and "the genital center has its symmetrical counterpart in the upper center of the throat" (p. 104); "reality is best and most fully apprehended directly through the body's sensual centers, without mediation from the mind at all" (p. 95). "body/bodies" 25; "bodily" 0; "flesh" 2 (pp. 123, 184, both Pynchon's or Nabokov's). The ch1_ch3 read, section 6, has the full reading of that chapter.

#### 2.3.11 The interface as permeable membrane

pp. 179-187, all in the Pynchon chapter and all already quoted in the ch2 read, section 5. Counts: "interface" 23, every one between pp. 179 and 187; "permeable" 5 (pp. 181, 181, 182, 182, 184); "membrane" 4. The sentences: "interfaces are not barriers, but points of exchange, surfaces through which two orders of being can interpenetrate" (p. 181); "the interfaces are always permeable, given the right perspective" (p. 182); "If film is one permeable membrane, the Word is another" (p. 184); "What makes you think there is an interface?" (p. 187).

#### 2.2.4 Things found in passing

1. **The chapter cross-references are one too high, confirmed on the Cornell file.** p. 37 "As we shall see in Chapter 6, Borges" (ch. 5); p. 40 "As we shall see in Chapter 3, it is when the narrator" (Pirsig, ch. 2); p. 44 "the 'polarities' and 'fields' that we shall encounter in Chapter 4" (Lawrence, ch. 3); p. 49 "the discussion in Chapter 5" (*Ada*, ch. 4); p. 69 "the turning kaleidoscope that we imagined in Chapter 1" (the kaleidoscope is in the Introduction, pp. 19-20); pp. 86, 87, 95, 142, 143, 169 "Chapter 2" for the scientific-models chapter (ch. 1); p. 138 "As we saw in Chapter 5, the impetus of *Ada*" (ch. 4); p. 183 "As we saw in Chapter 6, Borges" (ch. 5). **The Preface, by contrast, uses the printed numbering**: "Chapters 3, 4, and 6 of this book appeared in slightly different form as articles" (p. 12) names Lawrence, Nabokov and Pynchon, which matches the journals given. So the Preface was renumbered and the chapters were not. Unnumbered references ("the next chapter", pp. 19, 20, 83; "the final chapter", p. 84) are right.
2. **"Marlowe" is printed.** p. 23, render-checked. Hayles's spelling of Conrad's narrator; Conrad's is Marlow. Already in the ch1_ch3 read's chase list; now settled.
3. **"valorizing", not "valorizes"**, p. 174 (section 2 above).
4. **The aphorism about what science is about is given to Heisenberg at p. 84 and to Bohr at p. 97**, without a note either time (section 3.7).
5. **Back-matter spellings.** The text layer has "Wharf" (p. 202) and "Lancws" (pp. 203, 206), but the render of p. 202 shows "Whorf" printed correctly; the body has "Lanczos" at pp. 45 and 48. The Preface text layer has "Thomas V argish" (p. 11) for Thomas Vargish and "Nancy Crumbine"; names in the acknowledgements should be checked against a render before use.
6. **Pirsig's Quality is read as Bohr's model**, p. 65: "a model very similar to the one Bohr proposed in his interpretation of the Uncertainty Principle." The two earlier reads do not record this sentence; it is the book's second application of the Bohr viewpoints argument to a novelist (the first is Pynchon, p. 54).
7. **The index's "Cultural matrix, 23-24" omits p. 26 and the p. 66 footnote.**
8. **The Preface already contains the sentence that Pirsig footnote 4 (p. 66) expands**: "if my argument is sound, there must be many texts manifesting the influence of the field concept, in many different disciplines" (p. 11).
9. **Two spellings of a Heisenberg title in the notes**: "*Physics and Beyond: Encounters and Conversations*" (p. 112 n. 1, p. 203) and "*Physics and Philosophy*" (p. 45 n. 23, p. 51 n. 35, p. 203) are two different books, both cited; the ch1 epigraph (p. 31) is from the second.

## Part 3. 1990, *Chaos Bound*, deep read

N. Katherine Hayles, *Chaos Bound: Orderly Disorder in Contemporary Literature and Science*. Ithaca and London: Cornell University Press, 1990. From the held file: copyright 1990 by Cornell University (PDF index 5); Preface signed "Iowa City, Iowa" (p. xvi); chapter 2 appeared earlier in *Literature and Science: Theory and Practice*, ed. Stuart Peterfreund (Northeastern, 1990); parts of chapter 7 in *SubStance* and in *One Culture*, ed. George Levine (Wisconsin, 1987); chapter 5 in *Science-Fiction Studies*; portions of chapters 7 and 8 in *New Literary History* (p. xv). Cornell paperback ISBN on the cover scan reads 9780801497018 (PDF index 1), to be confirmed against the copyright page, which the text layer does not give.

`K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\Chaos_Bound.pdf`

Read 30 Oct 2026, the whole book: Preface pp. xi-xvi, chapters 1 to 10 pp. 1-295, Index pp. 305-308. The Selected Bibliography pp. 297-304 was not read.

**Folio rule for this volume.** Printed page = PDF index minus 19 for the whole body (PDF index 20 is p. 1, index 314 is p. 295), and for the back matter (index 316 is p. 297, index 324 is p. 305). Roman-numeral front matter: PDF index 12 is p. xi. The rule was read from the text layer at every page and checked on renders of pp. 3, 4, 5, 114, 174, 266, 267, 276, 281, 284 and 285. Every page number below is a printed folio.

**The file is not what the inventory said.** The 30 Oct inventory recorded the text layer as native. The producer string is "Acrobat 11.0.2 Paper Capture Plug-in with ClearScan" (created 4 May 2013): this is a ClearScan reconstruction like *The Cosmic Web*, with the same kind of slips ("vttt" for viii, "IO" for 10, "r 2" for 12, "Adan" for Atlan throughout, "Bohɼ Nɽɾ" in the index). It renders legibly, so quotations can be checked on the page, and eleven pages have been. The inventory's text-layer test does not detect ClearScan, as its own NOTES row says; this is the second of the thirteen it has missed, and the remaining eleven producer strings should be read before any of them is called native.

---

### 3.1 What the Preface says the first book was, and what this one is

The Preface (pp. xi-xvi) is Hayles's own account of the relation between the two books, and it is the place to start for the spine.

> "SOME years ago I began to wonder why different disciplines, sufficiently distant from one another so that direct influence seems unlikely, should nevertheless focus on similar kinds of problems about the same time and base their formulations on isomorphic assumptions. It seemed to me then, and still does seem, that **the most plausible explanation is cultural**. Different disciplines are drawn to similar problems because the concerns underlying them are highly charged within a prevailing cultural context. Moreover, different disciplines base the theories they construct on similar presuppositions because these are the assumptions that guide the constitution of knowledge in a given **episteme**." (p. xi)

> "My first attempt to demonstrate this phenomenon focused on the field concept ... It further implied (and this was perhaps its most important consequence for literature) that **there is no exterior, objective viewpoint from which to observe, for one is always already within the field**, caught in and constituted through the very interactions that one is trying to describe. **Field models were associated with the emergence of inherent limits on what can be articulated.**" (pp. xi-xii)

> "*Chaos Bound* is both sequel and complement to *The Cosmic Web*. **The paradigm of orderly disorder may well prove to be as important to the second half of the century as the field concept was to the first half.** Moreover, it brings into focus aspects of complex systems which had begun to surface within field models but which could not be adequately accounted for by them." (p. xiii)

> "Especially notable is the increased emphasis in *Chaos Bound* on locating science and literature within contemporary culture. **The recurrent image I use to explain the complex interconnections of theory, technology, and culture is a feedback loop.** ... Literary texts and theories were also involved in this cycle, for they too were affected by technology at the same time that they were affecting it." (pp. xiii-xiv)

> "These similarities notwithstanding, **different disciplinary traditions can impute strikingly different values to isomorphic paradigms.**" (p. xiv)

So by her own account the 1984 "field notion of culture" is here restated as "the most plausible explanation is cultural", given Foucault's word "episteme", and given a new image, the feedback loop, in place of the 1984 matrix. The word "matrix" survives (section 3.3).

### 3.2 The field concept in 1990

The term "field concept" occurs once in the running text, and the occurrence is important: it is one of three epistemes Hayles says she lives in at once.

> "When I drive to the store or play volleyball, for example, I am a Newtonian ... When I wonder why the price of gasoline has risen ten cents this week, I contemplate the complex mutual interactions most economically expressed through **the field concept that also serves as a metaphor for global society**. When I watch fractal forms being constructed on a computer screen, I am aware of yet another kind of thinking, distinct from the other two ... In what episteme do I live? Not in any single epistemology, but in a complex space characterized by multiple strata and marked by innumerable fissures." (pp. 220-221)

The field survives in two other forms. As the **cultural field**, pp. 4-5 and 23 (section 3.3). And as a field of **vectors**: "the arrows in the field do not all point in the same direction" (p. 23); "The denaturing process can be thought of as **a set of vectors defining a textual field of play**. The more vectors that are energized in a particular text or site, the more dimensional complexity the field will have" (p. 294). Quantum field theory appears eight times (pp. 26, 157, 186, 216, 267, 268, 271, 291), and at p. 268 she separates her own position from the appropriation of it: "quantum field theory has often been held up as validating the view that language is inherently self-referential and ungrounded", whereas it "led to a very specific and limited sense of the uncertainty imposed by observing or speaking from within the field (defined by Planck's constant in the uncertainty principle)".

**Her own index files the cultural field under the field concept.** Index, p. 306: "Field concept, xi-xii, 4-6, 23". Pages 4-6 and 23 are the cultural-field passages. That is the book's own statement that the 1990 cultural field is the 1984 field concept applied to culture.

### 3.3 The cultural matrix and the cultural field, every occurrence

**"Cultural matrix" occurs five times, not once.** `HAYLES_field_theory_of_culture_uptake_2026-10-30.md` recorded p. 4. The whole-word count over pp. 1-295 gives pp. 3, 4, 114, 174 and 285, and all five were read in place; pp. 4, 114, 174 and 285 were read from renders.

| p. | The sentence |
|---|---|
| 3 | "This is a three-sided study, triangulating among chaos theory, poststructuralism, and contemporary fiction. **Also of concern is the cultural matrix from which all three sides emerge and with which they interact.**" |
| 4 | "They illustrate how feedback loops among theory, technology, and culture develop and expand into complex connections between literature and science which are **mediated through the cultural matrix**." |
| 114 | Of scientific visions such as Prigogine's: "They signify more than the research can demonstrate; and **it is this excess signification that produces and is produced by the cultural matrix.**" |
| 174 | "When there is this kind of complex interplay between science and culture, **science cannot be separated from the cultural matrix. Like literature, science is always already cultural and cannot be otherwise.**" |
| 285 | "With this turn the self-reflexive nature of my argument becomes explicit, for **postmodern theories are themselves part of the cultural matrix that I have described as engendering them.**" |

**"Cultural field(s)" occurs six times**: pp. 4 (three), 5 (two), 23. The mechanism passage, pp. 4-5, is already in the uptake file; the sentences around it that were not recorded there:

> "The dual emphasis on **cultural fields and disciplinary sites** implies a universe of discourse that is at once fragmented and unified. Cultural fields bespeak the interconnectedness of a world in which instantaneous global communication is a mundane reality; local differences acknowledge the power of specialization within contemporary organizations of knowledge." (p. 4)

> "The connections I explore among contemporary literature, critical theory, and science **are not generally explainable by direct influence**. Rather, they derive from the fact that writers, critics, and scientists, however specialized or esoteric their work, all share certain kinds of everyday experiences." (p. 4)

> "Here influence spreads out through **a diffuse network of everyday experiences** that range from reading *The New York Times* to using bank cards on automatic teller machines to watching MTV. When enough of the implications in these activities point in the same direction, **they create a cultural field within which certain questions or concepts become highly charged.**" (p. 4)

> "The examples illustrate how the cultural field can energize different kinds of reevaluations, because **the arrows in the field do not all point in the same direction**. They also suggest that there is **an interaction between the culture and the new paradigms, rather than a one-way flow of influence** from one to the other." (p. 23)

And the third name for the same thing, p. 3: "In this book, I argue that certain areas within the culture form what might be called **an archipelago of chaos**", with the island figure ("connected both through substrata they share and through the larger forces that brought them into being").

**Where the influence question is put directly, p. 17**: "Because I discussed the scientific models first, I may have given the impression that they stimulated the development of analogous theories in literature. This is almost certainly not the case. The literary theories appeared simultaneously with, or slightly before, cognate formations in science ... Even if I did not favor a cultural explanation, I would be thrown back upon it by the facts of the case, unless I were willing to grant that the correspondences are coincidence." She cites her own two forthcoming essays on influence in n. 8.

### 3.4 Articulation, reflexivity, recursion

"Articulat*" 19 times. The 1984 problem of articulation is named in the Preface ("inherent limits on what can be articulated", p. xii; "unambiguous connections between theory and observation, articulation and reality", p. xii) and then absorbed into **reflexivity**: "the realization that there are inherent limits on what can be spoken" of 1984 becomes "the embedding of language in itself, reflexivity became unavoidable" (p. xiii). The working terms are now "self-reflexive" (34 occurrences, concentrated in chapter 2's "self-reflexive metaphors", pp. 31-37, 48, 56-57) and "recursi*" (34, concentrated in chapters 6 to 10). The thesis of chapter 2: "at these moments science necessarily confronts the enfolding of language-as-object into its assumed stance as a metalanguage. That is to say, **at these moments science confronts its literariness**" (p. 35). The closing of the circle, p. 48: "**Social context affects language, language affects thought, thought affects social context. The circle is closed. Objectivity has given way to hermeneutics.**" And the book's own position inside its loop: "I am conscious that out of the nearly infinite number of events that have happened in this century, I have chosen some few dozen ... No, I have not described the social matrix" (p. 293); "a theory that is itself frankly a construction involved in a recursive loop with postmodern culture" (p. 294).

### 3.5 Constraints, and the position that will be named constrained constructivism in 1991

The term is absent. The position is set out at pp. 221-226 and given a name, **tropism**, which does not survive into later books (to be checked). The sentences:

> "I will pursue another tack, arguing that we can adjudicate the issue by recognizing that **reality cannot be identified in its positivity but must be inferred from the constraints applicable in a given situation.**" (p. 221)

> "no matter how gravity is conceived, no viable paradigm could predict that when someone steps off a cliff, she will remain spontaneously suspended in mid-air. This possibility is ruled out by the nature of physical reality ... Gravity, like any totalizing idea, is always and inevitably a representation; our entire perceived world consists always and only of representations. **Yet within the representations we construct, some are ruled out by physical constraints, others are not.**" (pp. 221-222)

> "The position I am describing abandons all attempts to arrive at foundationalist certainty ... it could aptly be called **tropism**. Unlike positivism, it invites cultural readings of science." (pp. 223-224)

> "A better metaphor than vision is **touch**, for in contrast to sight's universalizing tendencies, touch is always localized ... in our brushes with (not view or vision of) reality, it cannot be seen, but it can sometimes be felt as our representations push against the limits of the possible." (p. 224)

> "the difference between physical and social constraints lies in the fact that **physical constraints manifest themselves in isomorphic ways in different representations**, whereas social constraints are specific to the representation within which they occur." (p. 226)

> "**The search for knowledge in the theater of representation thus proceeds by nay-saying rather than yea-saying.** We cannot see reality in its positivity; we can only feel it through universal constraints operating upon local representations." (p. 226)

The same thought is found in Serres and quoted approvingly at p. 203: science "conditioned but unconditional"; "the truth content of science is a necessary but not sufficient condition to determine the final form of a scientific paradigm. Reality imposes constraints that cause some hypotheses to be verifiable, others not. But it does not determine which theories are actually realized or how they will be expressed." "Constrain*" 35 occurrences, 19 of them on pp. 221-226. The 1991 essay in *New Orleans Review* is not held; whether it restates these pages is a chase item.

### 3.6 The observer inside the field

Preface p. xi (above). Atlan's equivocation, p. 56: "How an 'autonomy-producing' equivocation is seen depends on where the observer is stationed. If she is inside the channel, the equivocation is an interference ... If she is outside the channel, however, she can see the effect on the system as a whole." Shannon and Brillouin at p. 58: "the crux of the disagreement lies in where the commentator positions himself with respect to the transmission process." The denaturing of language, p. 268: "positing an all-inclusive field meant there was no vantage point outside the field from which to speak." And of herself, p. 293: "I could not have achieved the vantage outside or above my culture which an 'objective' description would imply." "observer(s)" 13.

### 3.7 Bohr and complementarity in 1990

"Bohr" twice. p. 211, in a list ("After Einstein, Bohr, Planck, and Schrödinger, physicists learned to specify that Newtonian equations applied only at nonrelativistic speeds"). p. 267, the attribution: "they were either discredited or reinterpreted to accommodate the realization that, **as Niels Bohr observed, 'we are suspended in language.'**" with n. 2 "For a fuller treatment of these developments, see Hayles, 1984." Render-checked. So in 1990 the aphorism's owner is Bohr, by her own hand, and the 1984 p. 84 attribution to Heisenberg is not repeated. **The viewpoints argument of 1984 pp. 53-55 is not revisited.** "Complementar*" twice: the title of her own forthcoming essay, "Reconfiguring Literature and Science: From Supplementarity to Complementarity", in *First-Born Affinities*, ed. Privateer and Rousseau (p. 17 n. 8), and "two complementary dreams" (p. 243). The essay is not held and is the one place complementarity may be doing work for her in this period. The uncertainty principle appears in a new role, as the guarantor that fluctuation always exists for chaotic systems to amplify (pp. 13-14, 159-160), and as the "very specific and limited" sense of uncertainty she holds apart from deconstruction's (p. 268).

### 3.8 Isomorphism, extended

"Isomorph*" 9 in running text, plus the Preface (xi, xiv). The 1984 method word is now given its own theory, in three steps. Why theories are isomorphic without influence, p. 184: "The two theories appear isomorphic not because they are derived from a common source or because they influenced each other, but because **their central ideas form an interconnected network, each part of which leads to every other part** ... Derrida and Feigenbaum entered the network at different points and for different reasons." The model for that, pp. 185-186, is Lem's: conceptual ecologies "can be modeled as closed topological spaces. Within a given topology, only certain forms are possible ... Not every possible form will be realized; particularities of history and personality determine which actually appear"; hence "**an ecology of ideas**" (pp. 176, 185; the phrase is Bateson's, n. 1 p. 176). Why isomorphic theories are valued differently, p. 190: "conceptually isomorphic theories take on different values when they are embedded in disciplinary matrices. Meaning is always dependent on context." And isomorphism as the mark of the physical, p. 226 (section 3.5). The three terms of chapter 7 are "ecology, economy, and equivocation" (p. 208).

### 3.9 Influence, resonance, affect

The anti-causal position of 1984 is now a mechanism: the feedback loop (xiv, 4, 271, 285-287, 289, 291, 295) and **resonance** ("until a resonance built up which was strong enough to be a contributing factor in his work", p. 5; "complex fields of intertextual resonances that affect signification", p. 19; "If these resonances had not existed, I think it unlikely that chaos theory would have aroused the widespread interest that it has", p. 23). "resonan*" 8.

**Affect, counted and read.** "affect" exact 12 (pp. 12, 12, 14, 15, 19, 24, 25, 136, 147, 193, 218, 221), "affects" 6, "affecting" and "affected" 9; **all 27 are the verb**, and the objects are weather patterns, flow, signification, understanding, the destination of a message, paradigms, our vision of the world. The noun "affect": 0. "Affective": 0. "Emotion*" 4 (pp. 111, 249, 251, 252), "feeling(s)" 12. Denominator 111,966 words, pp. 1-295, footnotes included. Two books, no noun.

### 3.10 The body, the cyborg, the posthuman: first appearance of the word

"Posthuman" occurs three times, the first uses in the run, all in chapter 10 and all render-checked:

> "The human subject who stands as the putative source of experience is also deconstructed and then reconstructed in ways that fundamentally alter what it means to be human. **The postmodern anticipates and implies the posthuman.**" (p. 266)

> "The prospect that human beings can become simulacra suggests that a new social context is emerging which will change not only what it means to be in the world but what it means to be human. Within this context-of-no-context, **the postmodern shades into the posthuman.**" (p. 276)

> "The point I wish to underscore here is not Haraway's ironic claim that the cyborg can be liberating but **her vision of the postmodern as the posthuman**. The cyborg is the specific instance for which the denaturing process is the general case." (p. 284)

The frame is **denaturing**: "I define cultural postmodernism as the realization that what has always been thought of as the essential, unvarying components of human experience are not natural facts of life but social constructions" (p. 265); four waves, language, context, time, "the denaturing of the *human*" (p. 266). The body enters through information: organisms "viewed as informational texts that can be opened to a literal embodiment of intertextuality by a variety of gene-splicing techniques. Such techniques point to **the deconstruction of the body as text**" (p. 272); "cyborgs ... already exist and are not particularly uncommon. About 10 percent of the U.S. population are cyborgs" (pp. 276-277); Gibson's video-game player "already a cyborg, for he is joined to the computer by a continuous interplay between his neural system and the computer's circuitry" (p. 277); "to see the human form as provisional" (p. 282). The ambivalence is stated: "What will happen to the movement for human rights when the human is regarded as a construction like any other?" (p. 285). "Embodiment" 3 (pp. 178, 241, 272), still instantiation; "body/bodies" 20; "bodily" 0; "corporeal*" 1 (p. 170). Haraway, pp. 282-284, is the first appearance of Haraway in the run.

### 3.11 Information, entropy, the separation of information from meaning

The ground of the book and of the next: "information" 303, "entropy" 124, "Shannon" 121, "Prigogine" 74, "cybernetic*" 21, "Wiener" 6. The thesis sentences: "a crucial move in the transvaluation of chaos was **the separation of information from meaning**" (p. 6); "Shannon's Choice", the decision "to equate information with entropy, rather than to oppose them" (p. 32); "For the alliance between entropy and information to be effective, information first had to be divorced from meaning (a premise made explicit in Shannon's 1948 papers)" (p. 51); "**World War II made information real**" (p. 269); Shannon "cut through the complexities of context dependence by the simple expedient of declaring that information had nothing to do with meaning. By implication, informational texts were also cut loose from their dependence on contexts" (p. 270). Cognition is not yet a theme: "cognit*" 2 (pp. 117, 229), "nonconscious" 0, "species" 2 incidental (pp. 98, 259), "nonhuman" 1 ("transcendent, nonhuman viewpoint", p. 134). The human sensorium is named once as a limit on representation: "such fundamental givens as the human sensory apparatus and neurophysiology" (p. 223), and Lem's scientists "can never escape their anthropomorphic perspective, encoded not only into the symbol systems but into the very structure of their brains" (p. 133).

### 3.12 Gender, and chaos as feminine

Chapter 6, pp. 171-174, on Gleick's *Chaos*: "this book has no women in it" (p. 171); "the world of science is first of all genderless, genderless because there is only one gender" (p. 172); "Chaotic unpredictability and nonlinear thinking, however, are just the aspects of life that have tended to be culturally encoded as feminine. Indeed, chaos itself has often been depicted as female" (p. 173); "By admitting the feminine as an abstract principle but excluding actual women, Gleick attains control over the polysemy of chaos ... science remains as monolithically masculine as ever" (p. 174). Keller is cited for this (n. 12, p. 171), with Bleier and Harding. On Lessing: "Doris Lessing is not a feminist, and *The Golden Notebook* is not a feminist novel" (Libby, quoted p. 247) and "I agree with these conclusions" (p. 247). Haraway's cyborg, pp. 282-284, with the verdict that "the cyborg, *as an actuality*, is deeply bound up with what Haraway calls the 'informatics of domination'" (p. 284). "feminis*" 8, "gender*" 9, "woman/women" 73.

### 3.14 Things found in passing

1. **The file is ClearScan**, not native (header above). The inventory CSV's judgement for this volume is wrong in the same way its *Cosmic Web* judgement was.
2. **The index files the cultural-field pages under "Field concept"** (p. 306: "Field concept, xi-xii, 4-6, 23"). There is no index entry for "cultural matrix" or "cultural field".
3. **The text layer prints Henri Atlan as "Adan"** at every occurrence (pp. 56, 205, 206, index p. 305); the render of p. 205 was not made, so the printed spelling is unconfirmed, but the bibliography key she gives is Atlan (1974) and the name should be taken as Atlan.
4. **Chapter cross-references are correct in this book** (checked: "chapter 4" for Prigogine, "chapter 6" for Feigenbaum, "chapter 2" for Shannon, "chapter 7" for Serres, "chapter 8" for the local and global, "chapter 9" for the simulacrum), unlike the 1984 book.
5. **Her own forthcoming work cited in the notes**, none of it held: "Reconfiguring Literature and Science: From Supplementarity to Complementarity" (p. 17 n. 8); "Turbulence in Literature and Science: Questions of Influence" (p. 17 n. 8); "Disciplinarity and the University: What It Keeps Us from Seeing" (p. 181 n. 5); and the edited *Chaos and Order* (pp. 207 n. 16, 233 n. 10), which is the next volume.
6. **The de Man note**, p. 230 n. 9: "This chapter was written before de Man's reviews in *Le Soir* surfaced."
7. **The Bonaventure footnote**, p. 277 n. 5: "The correct name of the hotel is the Bonaventura. For consistency, however, I will follow Jameson's usage."
8. **Positivism is told twice**: the 1984 account (pp. 37-40 there) is retold at pp. 222-223 and 267, with Feyerabend added to Kuhn and Hanson and Polanyi dropped.

## Part 4. 1991, *Chaos and Order* (edited), her Introduction

N. Katherine Hayles, ed., *Chaos and Order: Complex Dynamics in Literature and Science*. Chicago and London: University of Chicago Press, 1991. New Practices of Inquiry, series edited by Donald N. McCloskey and John S. Nelson. ISBN 0-226-32144-4 (paper). Copyright 1991 by The University of Chicago (PDF index 4, read from the text layer; the paper ISBN is the only one the page gives).

`K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\Hayles_N_Katherine_ed_Chaos_and_Order_Complex_Dynamics_in_Literature_and_Science_1991(1).pdf`

**Her voice in this volume is the Introduction, "Complex Dynamics in Literature and Science", pp. 1-33** (notes pp. 30-31, works cited pp. 31-33), and the Acknowledgments, p. vii. Read 30 Oct 2026 in full. The twelve contributors' chapters (pp. 37-306) were **not read**; they were scanned for a handful of terms only (section 4.6), to record where the contributors take up her own concepts. Every count in this part is over the Introduction alone unless the whole volume is named.

**Folio rule for this volume.** Printed page = PDF index minus 8 for the body (index 9 is p. 1, index 306 is p. 298); the file carries an embedded table of contents at the same offset. Producer "Adobe Acrobat 9.55 Paper Capture Plug-in" over an "Acrobat 4.0 Import" scan (created 2006): an OCR'd page-image scan, not ClearScan, so the page images are the originals and a render is a photograph of the print. Text-layer slips of the usual OCR kind ("Signals", "logiC", "chao tics" for chaotics, "Thorn" for Thom). Renders of pp. 1, 7 and 11 were read.

---

### 4.1 What the Introduction does

It sets out "chaotics", a term she takes from Ihab Hassan (p. 7, n. 8 "Private communication"), for the cultural phenomenon of which the science of chaos is one site; summarises the two branches of chaos theory (pp. 8-14); argues that linearity and nonlinearity carry values as well as denotations (pp. 16-18); lists the questions any literature-and-science argument must answer (p. 19); and introduces the twelve chapters (pp. 20-30). The spine concepts appear as follows.

### 4.2 The cultural matrix, twice, and the field as "the cultural matrix as a whole"

> "All of these factors, and more, contributed to **the cultural matrix out of which the science of chaos grew**. It should not be surprising, then, to find other sites within the culture that also embody the presuppositions informing chaos theory." (p. 7, render-checked)

> "The parallels, then, are extensive, so extensive that **the most likely source for them is no single site but the cultural matrix as a whole.**" (p. 11, render-checked)

"Cultural field", "societal matrix", "climate of opinion", "field theory of culture": 0 in the Introduction. "Field concept": 0; "field" 8 (pp. 1, 1, 2, 5, 13, 14, 25, 30), of which only p. 25 ("such field theories as relativity and quantum mechanics") is the physics sense. The 1984 concept is therefore carried in 1991 by "cultural matrix" alone, plus the Preface vocabulary of 1990 ("context", "milieu", "site").

### 4.3 Influence refused, and the mechanism named again

> "The question of how such isomorphisms arise is not easily answered. Let me say at the outset, however, that **I do not assume they are the result of direct influence between one site and another.** In particular, I am not arguing that the science of chaos is the originary site from which chaotics emanates into the culture. Rather, both the literary and scientific manifestations of chaotics are **involved in feedback loops with the culture. They help to create the context that energizes the questions they ask; at the same time, they also ask questions energized by the context.**" (p. 7)

> "The postmodern milieu does not necessarily cause articulations such as Prigogine's theory or Lem's novel in any direct sense. Rather, **it creates a context in which they become thinkable thoughts.**" (p. 14; the phrase repeats *Chaos Bound* p. 3, "thinkable, perhaps inevitable, thoughts")

> "Such an approach implies, of course, that the science of chaos is part of the culture, and that scientists, like everyone else, are **affected by the culture in which they are immersed.**" (p. 4)

And the list of questions that any such argument owes, p. 19, which is the nearest thing in the run so far to a specification of what the 1984 field theory of culture would have to do:

> "In the past, studies in literature and science have tended to follow a characteristic pattern. First some scientific theory or result is explained; then parallels are drawn (or constructed) between it and literary texts; then the author says in effect Q.E.D., and the paper is finished. In my view, every time this formula is used it should be challenged: **What do the parallels signify? How do you explain their existence? What mechanisms do you postulate to account for them? What keeps the selection of some theoretical features and some literary texts from being capricious?** What are the presuppositions of the explanations you construct, and how do they connect with what you are trying to explain?" (p. 19)

"isomorph*" 1 (p. 7). "resonan*" 1 (p. 14, Prigogine's "resonances with the emerging field of cultural studies").

### 4.4 Articulation restated, through Cartwright

The 1984 problem returns under Nancy Cartwright's camellias (pp. 16-17): "From the system's point of view, there is only the totality that is its environment. So strong is our belief in analysis, however, that we take the environment to be the artifact and the collection of factors to be the reality" (pp. 16-17); and then in her own voice, "(I observe in passing that I have analyzed the situation in terms of separate factors, whereas in reality they are one environment interacting continuously and holistically. **One can speak only in the language one has inherited.**)" (p. 17). Also p. 5: language "shapes even as it articulates thought"; "**As soon as discovery is communicated through language, it is also constituted by language.**" "articulat*" 7.

### 4.6 Her concepts in the contributors' chapters (term scan only, not a reading)

Whole volume pp. 1-298, 124,204 words. **"Cultural matrix" is used by two contributors in their own voice**: Linda K. Hughes and Michael Lund, "the critic's task is not to specify a Muse but to comprehend a shared musing, a meditative absorption in a cultural matrix" (p. 196); Adalaide Morris, of H.D., "Her first sustained explorations of this cultural matrix occur in *HERmione*" (p. 204). These are uptakes of the term inside the volume she edited, fourteen years before Rossini 2005, which the uptake file records as the one pickup found. **"Field concept"** is used by Hughes and Lund (p. 170, "according to the field concept everything (including the observer) is connected to everything else", attributed to Hayles pp. 15-59) and by Thomas Weissert (pp. 238, 241, citing *Cosmic Web* pp. 45-49 and 138-167 on Borges). **"Cosmic web"** 13 times in the volume, 7 of them bibliographic; the substantive uses are Hughes and Lund (p. 170), Morris (p. 210, "the articulations of this cosmic web", and Einstein's "Theory of Invariance", *Cosmic Web* 45), Weissert (p. 238) and Eric White (p. 271, "one cannot 'speak from within a field'"). "Bohr" 6 and "complementar*" 8 in the volume are the contributors' (Porush, Weissert, White), not read. None of this is Hayles's own voice; it is recorded because it is the earliest uptake of her 1984 terms in the corpus.

### 4.7 Found in passing

1. **Two 1989 essays of hers are cited and not held**: "Chaos as Orderly Disorder: Shifting Ground in Contemporary Literature and Science", *New Literary History* 20 (1989): 305-22 (n. 13), and "Text Out of Context: Postmodernism in an Information Society", *Discourse* 9 (1989): 24-36 (n. 7). Both fall between the first two books and the second is the source of the 1990 "denatured context" argument.
2. **Chaotics is Hassan's word**, by private communication (n. 8); it does not appear in *Chaos Bound*.
3. **The text layer prints René Thom as "Thorn"** (pp. 12, 33) and Ihab Hassan's note correctly; "chaotics" is split "chao tics" at most occurrences.
4. The works cited gives Jameson's "Postmodernism, or the Cultural Logic of Late Capitalism" as *New Left Review* 14 (1984): 653-92; the usual citation is *New Left Review* 146 (1984): 53-92. Check before copying.

## Part 5. 1999, *How We Became Posthuman*

N. Katherine Hayles, *How We Became Posthuman: Virtual Bodies in Cybernetics, Literature, and Informatics*. Chicago and London: University of Chicago Press, 1999. ISBN (cloth) 0-226-32145-2, (paper) 0-226-32146-0. Copyright 1999 by The University of Chicago (copyright page PDF index 4, read from a render).

`K:\OneDrive\UNM\Comps\Used texts\Hayles_N_Katherine_How_We_Became_Posthuman_Virtual_Bodies_in_Cybernetics_Literature_and_Informatics.pdf`

**Read 30 Oct 2026 in full**: Acknowledgments and Prologue (pp. ix-xiv), chapters 1 to 11 (pp. 1-291). Notes (pp. 293-323) and index (pp. 325-350) were term-scanned, not read. 366 PDF pages, 155,915 words in the whole file.

**Folio rule for this volume.** Printed page = PDF index minus 14 for the body (index 15 is p. 1, index 305 is p. 291); the file carries an embedded table of contents at the same offset. The metadata is empty (no producer string). Each page is one page image with a Times text layer behind it, so this is an OCR'd page-image scan, not a ClearScan reconstruction: a render is a photograph of the print, and the text layer carries the usual OCR slips ("vVeizenbaum", "IIacking", "fOCUSing", "Codel" for Godel, "Dr. Bloodrrwney", "Temlinal Games", "Adan" is not here). Renders of pp. 2, 3, 49, 196 and 288 and of the copyright page were read; everything else is checked against the text layer.

---

### 5.1 What the book does, in her own frame

Three stories (p. 2): "how information lost its body", "how the cyborg was created as a technological artifact and cultural icon", and "how a historically specific construction called the human is giving way to a different construction called the posthuman". Three waves of cybernetics (p. 7): homeostasis 1945-60, reflexivity 1960-80, virtuality 1980 on; each wave has an anchoring chapter on the science (3, 6, 9), one on an application (4, 8, 10) and one on literary texts (5, 7, 10) (pp. 20-21). The posthuman is defined by four assumptions (pp. 2-3, render-checked): informational pattern privileged over material instantiation; consciousness "an evolutionary upstart"; the body "the original prosthesis"; and "most important", that "the posthuman view configures human being so that it can be seamlessly articulated with intelligent machines" (p. 3). The book's wager is on the last clause of its own definition: "my dream is a version of the posthuman that embraces the possibilities of information technologies without being seduced by fantasies of unlimited power and disembodied immortality" (p. 5).

### 5.2 The field, and what replaces it

"Field concept", "field theory of culture", "cultural matrix", "cultural field", "societal matrix", "climate of opinion", "cosmic web": **all 0** in the body (126,745 words, pp. 1-291). "Field" 55, almost all ordinary ("the field of artificial life", "field glass"). The sense-bearing uses are:

| p. | Use |
|---|---|
| 15 | "we should be able to trace the development of a **conceptual field** by using a seriation chart analogous to the seriation charts used for artifacts" |
| 22 | chapter 9 "is organized by looking at this area of research as a **narrative field**" |
| 91 | Wiener: messages "constituted, measured, and communicated not as things-in-themselves but as **relational differences between elements in a field**. Communication is about relation, not essence" |
| 93 | "boundary formation and analogical linking collaborate to create a **discursive field** in which animals, humans, and machines can be treated as equivalent cybernetic systems" |
| 98 | "communication proceeds through selection from a **field of possible alternatives** rather than through the direct articulation of inherent reference" (Wiener and Saussure) |
| 196 | the "field" in which bodies take shape "may profitably be represented as an interplay between two intersecting axes ... the field itself is generated by the interplay *between* these end points" (render-checked; Grosz's word, taken over) |
| 221 | Maturana, Dick and Burroughs "explor[e] how to integrate observer and world into a **unified field of interaction**" |
| 251 | "The posthuman appears in these texts not as an abstract entity obeying general rules but as a **heterogeneous force field** through which certain vectors run" |

What does the 1984 field theory's job in 1999 is **seriation** (pp. 14-18), a term "appropriated from archaeological anthropology": "ideas were rarely made up out of whole cloth. Rather, they were fabricated in a pattern of overlapping replication and innovation, a pattern that I call 'seriation'" (p. 14); "Conceptual fields evolve similarly to material culture, in part because concept and artifact engage each other in continuous feedback loops" (p. 15). The historiographic claim is stated against both Kuhn and Foucault: "The development of cybernetics followed neither a Kuhnian model of incommensurable paradigms nor a Foucauldian model of sharp epistemic breaks" (p. 14). The mutual-influence sentence of the earlier books returns once, in the culture-and-science form: "culture circulates through science no less than science circulates through culture. The heart that keeps this circulatory system flowing is narrative" (p. 21). "Seriat*" 21; "constellation(s)" is the companion term (pp. 15-18, 51).

### 5.3 Articulation

"Articulat*" 57. Two uses carry weight. (a) The posthuman "can be seamlessly articulated with intelligent machines" (pp. 3, 34), and the Conclusion's "rethinking the articulation of humans with intelligent machines" (p. 287), with the limit stated: "there is a limit to how seamlessly humans can be articulated with intelligent machines, which remain distinctively different from humans in their embodiments" (p. 284). (b) The 1984 problem in its new dress, in chapter 8: embodiment cannot be articulated apart from the body, "not least because articulation systematizes and normalizes experiences in the act of naming them" (p. 197), and "Embodiment is akin to articulation in that it is inherently performative, subject to individual enactments, and therefore always to some extent improvisational" (p. 197); the task is "articulating embodiment and the body together" without "simply absorbing embodiment back into the body" (p. 198). Also p. 92: "the analogical relation constitutes both terms through the process of articulating their relationship".

### 5.4 Embodiment, the body, inscription, incorporation (the book's own theoretical frame, chapter 8)

"Embod*" 255, "embodiment" 120, "body/bodies" 486, "bodily" 16, "flesh" 33, "corporeal*" 11, "material*" 174.

> "Embodiment differs from the concept of the body in that the body is always normative relative to some set of criteria. ... In contrast to the body, embodiment is contextual, enmeshed within the specifics of place, time, physiology, and culture, which together compose enactment. Embodiment never coincides exactly with 'the body,' however that normalized concept is understood. Whereas the body is an idealized form that gestures toward a Platonic reality, embodiment is the specific instantiation generated from the noise of difference." (p. 196, render-checked)

> "as the body is to embodiment, so inscription is to incorporation." (p. 199)

> "Information, like humanity, cannot exist apart from the embodiment that brings it into being as a material entity in the world; and embodiment is always instantiated, local, and specific. Embodiment can be destroyed, but it cannot be replicated." (p. 49, render-checked)

> "embodiment makes clear that thought is a much broader cognitive function depending for its specificities on the embodied form enacting it." (p. xiv)

Five characteristics of incorporated knowledge (p. 205): contextual, sedimented and resistant to change, screened from consciousness, boundary-setting for conscious thought, and linked to new technologies: "Formed by technology at the same time that it creates technology, embodiment mediates between technology and discourse by creating new experiential frameworks that serve as boundary markers for the creation of corresponding discursive systems" (p. 205). Sources named: Merleau-Ponty, Connerton, Bourdieu (habitus), Dreyfus, Johnson, Grosz, Scarry, Varela/Thompson/Rosch. The 1984 "embodiment = instantiation" sense survives alongside the new one ("the concept it embodies", p. 15; "the frog's visual cortex" etc.), so a count of "embod*" cannot be read as a count of the concept. Against Foucault: "Foucault thus participates in, as well as deconstructs, the Panoptic move of disembodiment" (p. 194). The ch. 10 correction: Helen is not "disembodied (a state no presence in the world can achieve!) but rather ... her embodiment differs significantly from that of humans" (p. 265).

### 5.5 Flickering signifier (coined here) and skeuomorph (defined here)

> "Carrying the instabilities implicit in Lacanian floating signifiers one step further, information technologies create what I will call flickering signifiers, characterized by their tendency toward unexpected metamorphoses, attenuations, and dispersions. Flickering signifiers signal an important shift in the plate tectonics of language." (p. 30)

"In informatics, the signifier can no longer be understood as a single marker ... Rather it exists as a flexible chain of markers bound together by the arbitrary relations specified by the relevant codes" (p. 31); "A signifier on one level becomes a signified on the next-higher level" (p. 31); mutation is "the catastrophe in the pattern/randomness dialectic analogous to castration in the presence/absence dialectic" (p. 33). First use is the Prologue, p. xiv ("the flickering signifiers scrolling down the computer screens"). "Flickering signifier(s)" 21, "flickering signification" 5; recalled at p. 279. Chapter 2 is the 1993 *October* essay revised (p. x).

> "A skeuomorph is a design feature that is no longer functional in itself but that refers back to a feature that was functional at an earlier time." (p. 17; the Toyota Camry dashboard; "Skeuomorphs visibly testify to the social or psychological necessity for innovation to be tempered by replication")

"In the history of cybernetics, skeuomorphs acted as threshold devices, smoothing the transition between one conceptual constellation and another" (p. 17): homeostasis is the skeuomorph of the second wave, reflexivity of the third (pp. 17-18); figure 1, p. 16. "Skeuomorph*" 8, all pp. 13-17.

### 5.7 The first-person pages

The book's own method is performed in three first-person passages: the kitchen table, March 1996, reading the Brand photograph (pp. 80-83, "Janet Freud/Freed"); the UC San Diego music library, listening to Burroughs's tapes (p. 216); the UCLA laboratory conversations with Ray, Brooks and Tilden (pp. 225, 236-237). Each is placed where the argument says the observer must be put into the picture.

### 5.8 Found in passing

1. **Hassan's hyphen.** The chapter 1 epigraph prints "post-humanism" (p. 1); the chapter 10 quotation of the same sentence prints "posthumanism" (p. 247). Note 1 to chapter 1 (p. 293) distinguishes "the more radical idea of the posthuman (as distinct from posthumanism)". Check Hassan's 1977 text before quoting either form.
2. **Self-citations.** *Chaos Bound* is cited at ch. 2 n. 1 (pp. 31-60, the information-randomness paradox), ch. 2 n. 16, ch. 4 n. 44 (the reversal of entropy's sign, "chronicled elsewhere", p. 103), and ch. 10 n. 10 (pp. 56, 204-206, Atlan). *The Cosmic Web* is not cited anywhere in the book (0 in body and notes).
3. **"Tropism"** returns (pp. 164-165) as Wiener's word borrowed by Dick for the Moth and for his own attraction to "the dark-haired girl"; it is not the 1990 epistemic sense (*Chaos Bound* pp. 221-226).
4. **The Turing test chapter cross-reference.** p. xi cites the 1950 paper as "Computer Machinery and Intelligence"; the title is "Computing Machinery and Intelligence". Text layer only.
5. **"Chaotics"** does not survive into this book (0).
6. **The Latour sentence** (p. 291) and the "always already" (p. 279) are the two places "we have always been posthuman" is said; the first attributes the form to *We Have Never Been Modern*.
7. The 1999 chapters that were earlier articles are listed on pp. ix-x: ch. 2 (*October* 1993), ch. 3 part (*Configurations* 1994), ch. 4 part (*History of the Human Sciences* 1990), ch. 6 (*JFA* 1997), ch. 8 parts (*Configurations* 1993; *Sound States* 1997), ch. 9 (*FutureNatural* 1996), ch. 10 part (*Configurations* 1997). The ch. 8 embodiment/body frame is therefore datable to 1993.

### 5.10 To chase

1. Hassan, "Prometheus as Performer: Toward a Posthumanist Culture?" (1977), for the hyphen.
2. Hayles, "Virtual Bodies and Flickering Signifiers", *October* 66 (1993), for the first printing of the coinage, and "The Materiality of Informatics", *Configurations* 1 (1993), for the first printing of the body/embodiment frame.
3. Whether "seriation" survives into 2002 and 2005 as the historiographic term.
4. Whether "complementary", used here for pattern/presence, ever acquires Bohr's name in the later books.
