## Part 6. 2002, *Writing Machines*

N. Katherine Hayles, *Writing Machines*. Designed by Anne Burdick. Mediawork pamphlet series, edited by Peter Lunenfeld. Cambridge, Mass. and London: MIT Press, 2002. ISBN 0-262-58215-5. Library of Congress Control Number 2002113867. Copyright 2002 Massachusetts Institute of Technology (colophon p. 144, text layer).

`K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\Hayles_N_Katherine_Writing_Machines.pdf`

**Read 30 Oct 2026 in full**: Preface (pp. 4-7), chapters 1 to 8 (pp. 8-131), Lexicon Linkmap (pp. 72-73), Endtroduction by Peter Lunenfeld (pp. 138-139), Designer's Notes by Anne Burdick (pp. 140-141), Author's Acknowledgements (pp. 142-143), colophon (p. 144). 145 PDF pages, 34,057 words in the file; 31,781 words in the body pp. 4-131. The book has no notes: "the series format does not allow footnotes and other scholarly apparatus" (p. 143), and the apparatus was posted at the Mediawork site.

**Folio rule and a warning about this file.** Printed page = PDF index (offset 0 on 164 of 145 pages; the embedded table of contents agrees). Native publisher PDF (PDF 1.5, embedded fonts CIA-Earthworm, Cregyptian, EgyptienneF; created 2011), so the text layer is the typeset text and a render is the page as printed. **But the design sets some running text and all quoted "samples" from the three works as images**, so the text layer has gaps: pages 11-12, 15, 36-37, 43, 45, 56, 58-60, 62-63, 65, 68, 78, 80-87, 90-91, 93-97, 101, 104, 106-107, 109, 111-115, 117-123, 128, 131 have sentences that break off or resume mid-way, and the Lexicon pages (72-73) extract as a word list. Anything cited from those pages must be checked on a render. Renders of pp. 22, 31, 109 and 131 were read; the book's last paragraph (p. 131) exists only as an image and is quoted below from the render. Counts over the text layer are therefore undercounts, and the denominators below say so.

---

### 6.1 What the book does, in her own frame

"This book is an experiment in forging a vocabulary and set of critical practices responsive to the full spectrum of signifying components in print and electronic texts by grounding them in the materiality of the literary artifact" (p. 6). Three tutor texts: Memmott's *Lexia to Perplexia*, Phillips's *A Humument*, Danielewski's *House of Leaves* (p. 6). Two voices: the theoretical chapters (2, 4, 6, 8) and the narrative chapters (1, 3, 5, 7) carried by a persona, "Kaye", "a name related to mine but not the same" (p. 10), the device borrowed from J. Hillis Miller (p. 142); Burdick's note says the typeface is a synthesis of the two voices' faces, "Creegyptienne" (p. 141). Three terms are proposed: **material metaphor** (p. 22), **technotext** (p. 25), **media-specific analysis, MSA** (p. 29); a fourth, **inscription technology**, is defined (p. 24). The book's own account of its place in the run: *How We Became Posthuman* taught her "How materiality affected literature" (p. 7); the Endtroduction lists the four earlier books (p. 138, with the 1991 title misprinted, see 6.6).

### 6.2 The four terms, defined

> "To account for this traffic I propose *material metaphor*, a term that foregrounds the traffic between words and physical artifacts. We are not generally accustomed to think of a book as a material metaphor, but in fact it is an artifact whose physical properties and historical usages structure our interactions with it in ways obvious and subtle." (p. 22, render-checked; "traffic" is "between a symbol (more properly, a network of symbols) and material apparatus", "as old as the human species")

> "To count as an inscription technology, a device must initiate material changes that can be read as marks." (p. 24)

> "To name such works, I propose 'technotexts,' a term that connects the technology that produces texts to the texts' verbal constructions. ... 'Writing machines' names the inscription technologies that produce literary texts, including printing presses, computers, and other devices. 'Writing machines' is also what technotexts do when they bring into view the machinery that gives their verbal constructions physical reality." (pp. 25-26)

> "I want to call it media-specific analysis (MSA), as a way to invite theorists and critics to think more broadly about the connections between strands of criticism that have not yet made common cause with one another. Lulled into somnolence by five hundred years of print, literary studies have been slow to wake up to the importance of MSA." (p. 29)

> "Understanding literature as the interplay between form, content, and medium, MSA insists that texts must always be embodied to exist in the world. The materiality of those EMBODIMENTS interacts dynamically with linguistic, rhetorical, and literary practices to create the effects we call literature." (p. 31, render-checked)

Materiality itself is defined as emergent: "Materiality thus emerges from interactions between physical properties and a work's artistic strategies. For this reason, materiality cannot be specified in advance, as if it preexisted the specificity of the work. An emergent property, materiality depends on how the work mobilizes its resources as a physical artifact as well as on the user's interactions with the work" (p. 33); "In the broadest sense, materiality emerges from the dynamic interplay between the richness of a physically robust world and human intelligence as it crafts this physicality to create meaning" (p. 33). "materiality" 69, "material*" 172, "technotext*" 74, "material metaphor(s)" 34, "MSA / media-specific analysis" 34, "inscription technolog*" 14.

### 6.3 Embodiment in 2002

"embodiment" 13, "embod*" 21, "body/bodies" 42. The 1999 body/embodiment distinction is not restated; "embodiment" here is the text's instantiation in a medium (the 1984 sense returned, now of texts): "texts must always be embodied to exist in the world" (p. 31); Blake and Dickinson, "where embodiment is everything" (p. 32); "technotext has a body (or rather many bodies)" (p. 32); chapter 7's title "Embodiments of Material Metaphors" (p. 100). The reader's body enters through **MINDBODY** (pp. 48, 75): "The MINDBODY is engaged, not merely mind or body alone. Hence the force of material metaphors, for they control, direct, and amplify this traffic between the physical actions the work calls forth and structures, and the imaginative world the artifact creates" (p. 48); and through the sensory decoding of Memmott's puns, "the user needs three different sensory modalities: sight, sound, and kinesthesia" (p. 61); "The work can no more escape its body than its human interlocutors can escape theirs" (p. 62). Kaye on the artists' books: "Thinking makes shaping, shaping makes thinking" (p. 75), the "feedback loop from materiality to mind" that Bush's Memex neglects (p. 75), the only "feedback loop" in the book.

### 6.4 Subjectivity, the circuit, the posthuman in 2002

"subjectivity" 17, "posthuman" 3 (pp. 51, 130; p. 7 is the earlier book's title), "cybernetic*" 7, "distributed cognition" 2 (pp. 68, 72), "noise" 11. Memmott's work "insists on the co-originary status of SUBJECTIVITY and electronic technologies" (p. 49); "we metamorphose from individual interiorized subjectivities to actors exercising agency within the extended cognitive systems that include non-human actors" (p. 51); "In this posthuman conjunction, bodies of texts and bodies of subjects evolve together" (p. 51). The **remediated narrator** replaces the unreliable narrator: "a literary invention foregrounding a proliferation of inscription technologies that evacuate consciousness as the source of production and recover in its place a mediated subjectivity that cannot be conceived as an independent entity. Consciousness alone is no longer the relevant frame but rather consciousness fused with technologies of inscription" (pp. 116-117). The model: "the appropriate model for subjectivity is a communication circuit rather than discrete individualism, for narration remediation rather than representation, and for reading and writing inscription technology fused with consciousness" (p. 130), against Shannon's diagram, which positions humans "outside the channel" (p. 130). The last paragraph, image only, from the render of p. 131:

> "It is no accident that electronic texts such as *Lexia to Perplexia*, artists' books like *A Humument*, and print novels like *House of Leaves* envision subjects who are formed through and with the inscription technologies these works employ. The writing machines that physically create fictional subjects through inscriptions also connect us as readers to the interfaces, print and electronic, that transform us by reconfiguring our interactions with their materialities. Inscribing consequential fictions, writing machines reach through the inscriptions they write and that write them to re-define what it means to write, to read, and to be human." (p. 131, render)

### 6.6 Found in passing

1. **Lunenfeld's Endtroduction misnames the 1991 volume** "*Chaos and Disorder: Complex Dynamics in Literature and Science* (1990)" (p. 138); the title is *Chaos and Order* and the year 1991. Not her sentence; do not cite it for the bibliography.
2. **The persona.** Kaye's biography (Clarence, Missouri; Rochester Institute of Technology, chemistry; Caltech; the Ivy League first job; Iowa; UCLA; electrode programming in assembly code, pp. 10-16, 35) matches the author's public biography but the text forbids the identification: "no one should confuse her with me" (p. 10). Anything biographical taken from these chapters is Kaye's, not Hayles's, by the book's own rule.
3. **Chapter 4's "Alan Turing gave us the hint"** (p. 48) and the Macy "man in the middle" (p. 58) carry the 1999 book into this one; the "posthuman" appears twice in her voice (pp. 51, 130).
4. **The copyright-history argument** (pp. 31-32, Rose, Blackstone) is the 2002 form of "information lost its body": "print literature was widely regarded as not having a body, only a speaking mind" (p. 32).
5. **The theory passage** (pp. 104-106) is the one place in the run so far where she states her own view of theory's economics, recalling "Years ago I proposed" (p. 105); the earlier statement is not identified. Chase.
6. **Sources of chapters**: ch. 6 first in *Narrative*, ch. 4 in the SIGGRAPH Electronic Catalogue and *Digital Creativity*, ch. 8 in *American Literature* (p. 143).

## Part 7. 2004, *Nanoculture* (edited), her Introduction

N. Katherine Hayles, ed., *Nanoculture: Implications of the New Technoscience*. Graphic design by Danielle Foushée. Bristol and Portland: Intellect Books, 2004. ISBN 1-84150-113-1; electronic ISBN 1-84150-900-0. Copyright 2004 Intellect Ltd (copyright page PDF index 5, text layer; render read).

`K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\Nanoculture_ Implications of the New Techn - N. Katherine Hayles.pdf`

**Her voice in this volume is the Introduction, "Connecting the Quantum Dots: Nanotechscience and Culture", pp. 11-23**, with its 28 endnotes (pp. 203-204). Read 30 Oct 2026 in full. Roy Ascott's Preface (p. 9), the Acknowledgments (p. 10) and the eleven contributors' chapters (pp. 25-199) were **not read**; the contributors' chapters were term-scanned (section 7.5) for uptake of her own terms. Every count in this part is over the Introduction unless the whole volume is named.

**Folio rule for this volume.** Printed page = PDF index plus 2 for the body (index 9 is p. 11, index 21 is p. 23). Producer "Adobe Acrobat 7.08 Image Conversion Plug-in" (2006): a page-image scan with an OCR text layer, so a render is a photograph of the print. **The text layer drops every italic title** (the italic font is unmapped and extracts as control characters), so book titles inside the Introduction and the notes are missing from the extraction and were read from renders where cited below. Renders of pp. 11, 12, 22 and 23 and the copyright page were read.

---

### 7.1 What the Introduction does

A survey of the field (pp. 11-14): the single-electron transistor, colloidal data storage, quantum dots; Drexler, Feynman, the Foresight Institute, Zyvex; the mechanical versus biological metaphors for nanomachines (pp. 12-13); science fiction "as the Other of nano-technoscience" (p. 14). Then the LACMA *nano* exhibit and SINAPSE (pp. 15-17), and the chapters introduced one by one (pp. 16-23). The volume grew from a collaboration with Victoria Vesna and James Gimzewski (p. 15).

### 7.2 The field and the matrix

"Field concept", "field theory", "cultural matrix", "cultural field", "cosmic web": **0 in the Introduction**. "field" 0 in the sense (the OCR gives "ﬁ eld" with a ligature, so the whole-word count misses it; "ﬁ eld" occurs about fourteen times as "the field of nanotechnology", ordinary). The nearest statement is the last sentence of the Introduction, which carries the 1984 mutual-affect form:

> "We can best achieve this, I think, through constructive interventions that enable scientists and technologists, as well as the society generally, to understand more fully the connections that enweb science and technology within the cultural and social processes that **they affect even as they are affected by them**." (p. 23, render-checked)

And on metaphor, the 1990 topology recast as a track: "The choice of metaphor is consequential, for it lays down a linguistic track that thought tends to follow and suggests connections that bind new ideas into networks of existing conceptual structures" (p. 13); "One could almost say that the decision to locate micro-phenomena in the biological or mechanical realm is as much a function of the metaphors chosen as of the phenomena themselves" (p. 13).

### 7.3 Bohr, in her voice, for the first time since 1990

Summarising Susan Lewak's chapter (pp. 22-23, render-checked):

> "Drawing on Neils Bohr's understanding of the quantum world as a challenge to normative language based on macroscale experience, Lewak suggests that language and subjectivity are intimately related. Bohr argued that in the quantum world subject and object merge in what he called the 'quantum of action.' Rather than existing independent of the observer, as objects do in the macroworld, entities in the quantum realm come into existence as measurable phenomena simultaneously with the methods used to observe them. 'We are suspended in language,' Bohr famously observed, a maxim that Alice verifies for herself when she finds out that her macroscale language apparently does not work in Wonderland as it did in the ordinary world." (pp. 22-23)

Note 27 (p. 204): "Neils Bohr took over this term (its usual meaning is the numerical value of Planck's constant) to designate the point at which no further distinction between the observer and system is possible." Note 28: "This favorite saying of Bohr is documented by Aage Petersen, 'The Philosophy of Neils Bohr,' *Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists* 19 (September 1963), 10" (the journal title is read from the bibliography pattern; the italic is unmapped). This is the same aphorism she gave at *Cosmic Web* p. 97 and *Chaos Bound* p. 267 n. 2, now with its source. "Bohr" 3 in the Introduction (pp. 22, 22, 23), "complementar*" 0, "uncertainty" 0, "quantum" 9 (quantum dots, quantum tunnel, "quantum wave/particle dualities", p. 22). **The spelling "Neils" is printed** (render p. 22 and note 27, text layer); the name is Niels.

### 7.5 Her concepts in the contributors' chapters (term scan only, not a reading)

Whole volume pp. 25-245, 86,242 words of text layer. **"Cultural matrix" is used by Kate Marshall, citing *The Cosmic Web***: "They form part of what N. Katherine Hayles calls the 'cultural matrix' as a model for examining the forces which guide scientific and intellectual inquiry. Rather than drawing direct causal lines of influence, she says, an appropriate analysis of interdisciplinary exchange would have to consider the constellation of forces ..." (p. 144, n. 12 "See N. Katherine Hayles, [title unmapped] (Ithaca: Cornell University Press, 1984)"); and again "a cultural matrix thematized by convergence" (p. 146). This is a third uptake of the 1984 term, after Hughes and Lund and Morris (1991) and before Rossini (2005). "Field concept" 0 in the volume. "Hayles 1999" is cited by de Souza e Silva for Shannon's definition (p. 34). **Bohr and Barad together in Lewak**: "Niels Bohr made a similar determination about the activities at the quantum scale ... Bohr's argument leads to a new relationship between subject and object as indicated by Karen Barad when she argues that: [Agential Realism] is a theory of knowledge and reality whose fundamental premise is that reality consists of phenomena that are reconstituted in [intra-action] with the interventions of knowers" (p. 196; nn. 22-23 cite Barad, "Agential Realism: Feminist Interventions in Understanding Scientific Practices", and the *Encyclopedia of Feminist Theories* entry). Lewak spells the name "Niels"; Hayles's Introduction and notes spell it "Neils". "Bohr" 7 in the volume (Lewak, Landon's "Bohr Maker" p. 137, and the notes). None of this is Hayles's own voice; it is recorded because the volume she edited is the first place in the corpus where Barad's agential realism stands next to Bohr.

### 7.6 Found in passing

1. **"Neils Bohr"** is printed twice in her Introduction's apparatus (p. 22 body, p. 204 nn. 27-28, both "Neils"); Lewak's chapter has "Niels". Quote with [sic] or silently correct, but note it.
2. **The Bohr aphorism's source** is given here for the first time in the run: Aage Petersen, *Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists* 19 (September 1963), p. 10 (n. 28).
3. **The 2004 Introduction cites none of her own books**; its 28 notes are to nanotechnology sources, Moravec, Vinge, Bohr and Petersen.
4. **Table of contents versus text.** The contents page lists Kate Marshall's chapter as "Atomizing the Risk Technology" and the running title and Introduction call it "Future Present: Nanotechnology and the Scene of Risk" (p. 19); the contents list has "NATHAN BROW" for Brown (OCR). Jessica Pressman's "Nano Narrative" is introduced (p. 21) but does not appear in the embedded table of contents; check the printed contents before citing the chapter list.
5. **The italic font is unmapped in the file**, so every book title inside the Introduction (Drexler's *Engines of Creation*, *Nanosystems*, *Unbounding the Future*; Crichton's *Prey*; Bear's *Blood Music*; Stephenson's *The Diamond Age*; Houellebecq's *The Elementary Particles*; Egan's *Permutation City*; Loyer's *Chroma*) is absent from the text layer and must be read from a render.

## Part 8. 2005, *My Mother Was a Computer*

N. Katherine Hayles, *My Mother Was a Computer: Digital Subjects and Literary Texts*. Chicago and London: University of Chicago Press, 2005. ISBN 0-226-32147-9 (cloth), 0-226-32148-7 (paper). Copyright 2005 by The University of Chicago (copyright page PDF index 3, render read). Dedication: "To my mother, who was not a computer" (PDF index 4).

`K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\My Mother Was a Computer_ Digital Subjects - N. Katherine Hayles.pdf`

**Read 30 Oct 2026 in full**: Prologue (pp. 1-11), chapters 1 to 9 (pp. 15-240), Epilogue (pp. 241-243). Notes (pp. 245-264) scanned for her self-citations; Works Cited (pp. 265-278) and Index (pp. 279-?) term-scanned. 301 PDF pages, 129,351 words in the file; 106,910 words in the body pp. 1-243.

**Folio rule for this volume.** Printed page = PDF index minus 9 for the body (index 10 is p. 1, index 252 is p. 243). Producer "Adobe Acrobat 9.3 Paper Capture Plug-in with ClearScan" (creator "Canon iR C5058", 2010): a ClearScan reconstruction, as the 1984 and 1990 files are, so a render is a traced reconstruction of the print and not a photograph, and the text layer has ClearScan slips ("1930S" for 1930s, "oflanguage", "Zitek" for Zizek on pp. 228, 234, "Epsen" for Espen in the index, "In the Cage" headed "Code as Reinscription: 'n the Cage"). Renders of pp. 3, 7, 33, 103 and 142 and of the copyright page were read; the quotations below marked render-checked were read from those.

---

### 8.1 What the book does, in her own frame

"Writing Machines, How We Became Posthuman, and this book form a trilogy that arcs from the mid-twentieth century to the present, a trajectory that moves from a binary opposition between embodiment and information through an engagement with the materiality of literary texts to a broadening and deepening of these ideas into computation and textuality" (pp. 2-3, render-checked). The three parts are making (language and code), storing (print and electronic text), transmitting (analog and digital) (pp. 6-7). The frame is **the Regime of Computation** or **Computational Universe**, "the claim that the universe is generated through computational processes running on a vast computational mechanism underlying all of physical reality" (p. 3, render-checked), taken "simultaneously as means and metaphor" (p. 4), with the question "when the question remains undecidable" (p. 20) left open. The tutor texts: Wolfram, Fredkin, Morowitz (ch. 1); Saussure and Derrida against code (ch. 2); James, Dick, Tiptree (ch. 3); the William Blake Archive, McGann, TEI (ch. 4); *Cryptonomicon* (ch. 5); *Patchwork Girl* (ch. 6); Deleuze, Guattari, Lacan and Lem's "The Mask" (ch. 7); Karl Sims's virtual creatures and Jim Campbell (ch. 8); Egan's trilogy and Zizek (ch. 9).

### 8.2 Intermediation, defined three times

> "This entanglement of the bodies of texts and digital subjects is one manifestation of what I call 'intermediation,' that is, complex transactions between bodies and texts as well as between different forms of media." (p. 7, render-checked)

> "These complex and entangled interactions are what I call 'intermediation,' a term suggested by Nicholas Gessler. An important aspect of intermediation is the recursivity implicit in the coproduction and coevolution of multiple causalities. Complex feedback loops connect humans and machines, old technologies and new, language and code, analog processes and digital fragmentations." (p. 31)

> "Nevertheless, for my purposes I prefer the term 'intermediation.' 'Remediation' has the disadvantage of locating the starting point for the cycles in a particular locality and medium, whereas 'intermediation' is more faithful to the spirit of multiple causality in emphasizing interactions among media. ... I want to expand its denotations to include interactions between systems of representations, particularly language and code, as well as interactions between modes of representation, particularly analog and digital. Perhaps most importantly, 'intermediation' also denotes mediating interfaces connecting humans with the intelligent machines that are our collaborators in making, storing, and transmitting informational processes and objects." (p. 33, render-checked)

"intermediation" 43; "remediat*" named as Bolter and Grusin's (pp. 32-33). The 2002 "medial ecology" survives once as "media ecology" (p. 32: "a robust media ecology in which new media represent and are represented in old media") and p. 97 ("the dynamic media ecology of the twenty-first century").

### 8.3 Materiality, repositioned; "New Materialism", named

> "From my perspective, this development requires repositioning materiality as distinct from physicality and re-envisioning the material basis for hybrid texts and subjectivities." (p. 2)

> "Materiality, as I defined it in *Writing Machines*, is an emergent property created through dynamic interactions between physical characteristics and signifying strategies. Materiality thus marks a junction between physical reality and human intention. Following Bruno Latour's call for a turn from 'matters of fact' to 'matters of concern,' I like to think of materiality as the constructions of matter that matter for human meaning." (p. 3, render-checked)

> "*The materiality of an embodied text is the interaction of its physical characteristics with its signifying strategies.* Centered in the artifact, this notion of materiality extends beyond the individual object, for its physical characteristics are the result of the social, cultural, and technological processes that brought it into being." (p. 103, render-checked, italics in the original)

> "Whereas the New Criticism of the mid-twentieth century isolated texts from political contexts and technological productions, **the New Materialism I am advocating in this book and practicing in this chapter** insists that technologies and texts be understood as mutually interpenetrating and constituting one another." (p. 142, render-checked)

This is the only place in the run so far where she names her own position with the phrase; "materialis*" otherwise appears as "materialistic level of signification" (p. 175, of Guattari), "the materialist/discursive divide" (p. 209) and "cultural materialists" (p. 216). "material*" 151, "materiality" about 65. Also: "In the worldview of code, materiality matters" (p. 43); "The Regime of Computation presumes and requires materiality at the same time that it transforms our understanding of the nature of materiality" (p. 218); and ch. 4's "In my view, the ontology card is not worth playing. There is no Platonic reality of texts. There are only physical objects such as books and computers, foci of attention, and codes that entrain attention and organize material operations" (p. 97).

### 8.4 The field and its substitutes

"Field concept", "field theory", "cultural matrix", "cultural field", "cosmic web": **0**. "field" 18, all ordinary. **"Climate of opinion", the 1984 term, returns twice and is given to Raymond Williams**: whether computation "should be understood as a metaphor pervasive in our culture and therefore indicative of a certain 'climate of opinion' (in Raymond Williams's phrase) at the turn of the millennium, or whether it has ontological status" (p. 20); the theories' "roles in helping to create a 'climate of opinion,' as Raymond Williams calls it" (p. 172). (In 1984 the phrase was Whitehead's, *Cosmic Web* p. 22; check which Williams text she means, since "structure of feeling" is his usual phrase.) The account of cultural change is now "feedback loops that connect culturally potent metaphors with social constructions of reality" (p. 20), "recursive, multiple causalities" (p. 4), and Egan's aphorism as hers: "what we make and what (we think) we are coevolve" (p. 216; pp. 240, 242, 243). The nearest to a field statement is the Epilogue: "boundaries of all kinds have become permeable to the supposed other. Code permeates language and is permeated by it; electronic text permeates print; computational processes permeate biological organisms; intelligent machines permeate flesh. ... Boundaries are both permeable and meaningful" (p. 242).

### 8.5 Embodiment, body, subject in 2005

"embodiment" 32, "embod*" 107, "body/bodies" 261. The book begins by loosening the 1999 frame: "a binary view that juxtaposes disembodied information with an embodied human lifeworld is no longer sufficient to account for these complexities. Although I have not abandoned my commitment to the importance of embodiment, it seems to me that contemporary conditions call increasingly for understandings that go beyond a binary view to more nuanced analyses" (p. 2). "human mindbodies" (p. 7, render-checked). The body/embodiment and inscription/incorporation pairs of 1999 are cited in a note (ch. 8 n. 34, p. 260) rather than restated. New pairs: **analog subject and digital subject** (Poster, pp. 201-204): the analog subject "a depth model of interiority", the digital subject "an emergent complexity ... a dynamic of fragmentation and recombination" (p. 203); the "Oreo" (p. 207), analog at the bottom and top, digital in the middle; "from form to process" (pp. 209-211): "I find it useful to think of them as processes rather than as bodies" (p. 210); "I think, therefore I connect with all the other cognizers in my environment, human and nonhuman" (p. 213). Epilogue: "fleshly creatures are composed of embodiments that differ qualitatively from artificial life forms" (p. 242). Grosz's Moebius strip returns (p. 160); Keller's "hard work to get nature to obey the laws of nature" for the third time in the run (p. 208; also 2002 p. 14).

### 8.7 Her self-citations

*How We Became Posthuman*: ch. 3 nn. 10, 25 (pattern/randomness; Dick's gender politics); ch. 8 nn. 28, 34, 42 (artificial life pp. 222-46; "incorporation and inscription as two alternating ways of understanding written documents"; the flickering signifier). *Writing Machines*: ch. 4 n. 24 and ch. 8 n. 34. *Chaos Bound* and *The Cosmic Web*: 0 in the notes. ### 8.8 Found in passing

1. **"New Materialism"** as her own name for her position (p. 142), two years before the term became a label for Barad, Bennett, Coole and Frost. Nothing in the book connects it to Barad (0) or to Deleuze beyond the Body without Organs. Live for the proposal.
2. **"Climate of opinion"** reattributed to Raymond Williams (pp. 20, 172); in 1984 it was Whitehead's (*Cosmic Web* p. 22). Check Williams.
3. **"Third-order cybernetics"** proposed as a name for emergence (p. 218), with the reason she does not use it: the Regime of Computation "presumes and requires materiality".
4. **"The Oreo"** (p. 207) and **"Work as Assemblage" / "WaA"** (p. 105), two coinages that do not appear in the 2004 or 2002 volumes; whether they survive is for the later books.
5. **The first-person method continues**: "I think, therefore I connect" (p. 213); "the yellow legal pad on which I scribble notes as I peer at the screen" (p. 211); "my salad days recede in the distance" (p. 164); "I would like not to like Greg Egan's fiction" (p. 214).
6. **ClearScan slips to correct in any quotation**: "Zitek" (pp. 228, 234); "1930S", "1950S"; "oflanguage"; "'n the Cage" (p. 65 heading); "Lac an" (p. 176).
7. **Egan's and Zizek's names**: the text layer has no diacritics for Zizek; the print has them.

## Part 9. 2008, *Electronic Literature*

N. Katherine Hayles, *Electronic Literature: New Horizons for the Literary*. University of Notre Dame Ward-Phillips Lectures in English Language and Literature. Notre Dame, Indiana: University of Notre Dame Press, 2008. ISBN 978-0-268-03084-1 (cloth), 978-0-268-03085-8 (paper). Copyright 2008 by University of Notre Dame (copyright page PDF index 4, text layer; render made). With a CD-ROM, *Electronic Literature Collection*, volume 1, edited by Hayles, Nick Montfort, Scott Rettberg and Stephanie Strickland, and a companion site.

`K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\Electronic Literature_ New Hori - N. Katherine Hayles.pdf`

**Read 30 Oct 2026 in full**: Read Me (pp. ix-xiv), chapters 1 to 5 (pp. 1-186). Notes (pp. 187-210) scanned for her self-citations and the hyper attention source; index (pp. 211 on) not read. 240 PDF pages, 65,482 words in the file; 50,910 words in the body pp. 1-186.

**Folio rule for this volume.** Printed page = PDF index minus 14 for the body (index 15 is p. 1, index 200 is p. 186). Native publisher PDF (QuarkXPress 6.5, created January 2008): the text layer is the typeset text, so no OCR slips; renders of pp. 45, 117 and 135 were made as a check and agree with the text layer. The one extraction artefact is the ligature "ﬁ" ("ﬁeld", "speciﬁcity"), which defeats whole-word counts for words containing it; counts for such words are given from a ligature-normalised pass where stated.

---

### 9.1 What the book does, in her own frame

A teaching book: "intended to help electronic literature move into the classroom" (p. ix). Chapter 1 is "the first attempt to survey systematically the entire field of electronic literature" (p. ix), the ELO's definition quoted (p. 3) and "the literary" proposed as the wider category, "creative artworks that interrogate the histories, contexts, and productions of literature, including as well the verbal art of literature proper" (p. 4). Chapter 2 is intermediation (from *New Literary History* 2007); chapter 3 is Kittler against Hansen, "the body and the machine"; chapter 4 "how electronic literature revalues computational practice"; chapter 5 the print novel and "the mark of the digital". Four of the five chapters are revised articles (p. xiii). Thesis sentences: "Literature in the twenty-first century is computational" (p. 43); "digitality has become the textual condition of twenty-first-century literature" (p. 186).

### 9.2 Intermediation, now a theory of cognition

> "One proposal is 'intermediation,' a term I have adopted from Nicholas Gessler, whereby a first-level emergent pattern is captured in another medium and re-represented with the primitives of the new medium, which leads to an emergent result captured in turn by yet another medium, and so forth. The result is what researchers in artificial life call a 'dynamic hierarchy' ... Because these interactions go up as well as down, down as well as up, such a system might more appropriately be called a 'dynamic heterarchy.'" (p. 45, render read)

"Humans engineer computers and computers reengineer humans in systems bound together by recursive feedback and feedforward loops" (p. 48); Hofstadter's "Cognition is recognition" (p. 48) and the "analogy-between-analogies" (pp. 49, 70, 74); Fredkin's "the meaning of information is given by the process that interprets it" (p. 52) generalised into "cascading processes of interpretation" (pp. 53, 57, 119); MacKay and Shannon reconciled through it (pp. 54-55); the "meta-analogy: as human cognition is to the creation and consumption of the work, so computer cognition is to its execution and performance" (p. 57). "intermediation" 54; chapter 2 is the heaviest use in the run. "the Regime of Computation" and "Computational Universe" of 2005 do not appear by name; "Literature in the twenty-first century is computational" (p. 43) takes their place.

### 9.3 Hyper attention and deep attention, named in a book for the first time

> "This cognitive mode, which I have elsewhere called 'hyper attention,' is distinctively different from that traditionally associated with the humanities, which by contrast can be called 'deep attention.' Deep attention is characterized by a willingness to spend long hours with a single artifact (for instance, a seven-hundred-page Victorian novel), intense concentration that tends to shut out external stimuli, a preference for a single data stream rather than multiple inputs, and the subvocalization that typically activates and enlivens the reading of print literature." (p. 117, render read)

Hyper attention: "a craving for continuously varying stimuli, a low threshold for boredom, the ability to process multiple information streams simultaneously, and a quick intuitive grasp of algorithmic procedures that underlie and generate surface complexity" (p. 117). Source: n. 20, "Hyper and Deep Attention: The Generational Divide in Cognitive Modes," *Profession* 2007 (p. 204). The pair is set inside neural plasticity, synaptogenesis and the Baldwin Effect (pp. 113-116), Deacon's *Symbolic Species* (p. 115), Ambrose on compound tools and Broca's area (p. 113), the Kaiser "Generation M" report (pp. 114, 117). "hyper attention" 11, "deep attention" 11 in the body (pp. 117-128, 176-177); "attention" 34.

### 9.4 The technological nonconscious, and "nonconscious" four times

> "While building on Thrift's insights, I prefer to use the term 'technological nonconscious' to avoid confusing the sedimented embodied experiences he discusses with the Freudian unconscious. I also diverge from Thrift in my emphasis on computational technologies. ... I argue that while the technological nonconscious has been a factor in constituting humans for millennia, the new cognitive capabilities and agencies of intelligent machines give it greater impact and intensity than ever before." (p. 135, render read)

Thrift's "technological unconscious" (p. 134) is the source; the term returns at p. 157: computation "addresses us with the full complexity our human natures require, including the rationality of the conscious mind, the embodied response that joins cognition and emotion, and the technological nonconscious that operates through sedimented routines of habitual actions, gestures, and postures." "nonconscious" 4 (pp. 135 three times, 157); "subcognitive" is the companion word (pp. 51-57, 81, 184-185); "non- and subcognitive processes" (pp. 184-185); Dennett's "Multiple Drafts Model" and the self as "internal monologue" (p. 80). The 2005 "human nonconscious processes" has become a named term here, with "technological" in front of it.

### 9.5 Embodiment against Hansen and Kittler

Chapter 3 is the run's longest argument with Hansen (pp. 102-113): "both the body and machine orientations work through strategic erasures" (p. 88); "Embodiment will not become obsolete because it is essential to human being, but it can and does transform in relation to environmental selective pressures, particularly through interactions with technology" (p. 104); Ruyer's "transpatial domain" as "soul" (p. 105); "if the body and technology are involved in a coevolutionary spiral, neither logically has precedence over the other" (p. 108); the potter and the clay, Pickering's mangle (p. 109); "technologies are embodied because they have their own material specificities as central to understanding how they work as human physiology, psychology, and cognition are to understanding how (human) bodies work" (p. 112); "People and machines are both embodied, and the specificities of their embodiments can best be understood in the recursive dynamics whereby they coevolve with one another" (p. 129). The chapter's method statement: "Thus Kittler has it right in insisting on the catalyzing effects of media on the body ... Hansen has it right in insisting on the importance of embodiment" (p. 119); and against both rhetorics, "the model herein proposed entangles body and machine in open-ended recursivity with one another" (p. 130). "embodiment" 26, "embod*" 84, "body/bodies" 102. The 1999 body/embodiment pair is not restated; "mindbody" once (p. 137); the typing example recalled from HWBP (p. 133, n. 4 pp. 199-200). Bourdieu's habitus (pp. 132-133) and Damasio (pp. 133-134) return from 1999.

### 9.6 Technogenesis, borrowed from Hansen and kept

"technogenesis" 2, both in reporting Hansen: mixed reality as "a concrete moment in the history of human technogenesis in which the constituting or ontological dimension of embodiment is incontrovertibly exposed" (*Bodies in Code* 9, quoted p. 109); "Although Hansen pays homage to the idea of human-technological coevolution in employing the term 'technogenesis,' its implicit negation often creeps into the rhetorical formulations of his argument" (p. 110). The word is his here (and Stiegler's behind him); her own term for the same idea is "coevolution" ("human-technological coevolution", p. 110; "the coevolution of the body and technology", p. 109; "coevolutionary spiral", p. 108; "The logic of coevolution", p. 131).

### 9.9 Found in passing

1. **"Hyper attention" and "deep attention" enter the books here** (p. 117), one year after the *Profession* article; the next book (2012) will carry them as a chapter.
2. **"Technological nonconscious"** is coined here against Thrift's "technological unconscious" (p. 135); the 2017 title term is three books away.
3. **"Technogenesis" is Hansen's in this book** (pp. 109-110); she uses "coevolution". The 2012 subtitle adopts the word.
4. **The hypermasculinized traders** (pp. 99-102) are the one place in the run so far where gendered culture is argued as codetermining a media condition, with the counter-example of "tending a two-year-old" (p. 101).
5. **Her programming autobiography**: "the room-sized IBM 1401 machine on which I first learned to program (sporting all of 4K memory)" (p. 2).
6. **Chapter 3's typo in a citation**: "*How We Became Posthuman: Virtual Bodies in Cybernetics, Literature and Cybernetics*" (p. 133); the subtitle ends "and Informatics".
7. **The 1999 sense of "classical" and "contemporary"** for first- and second-generation electronic literature (p. 7) replaces her own 2002 "first-generation / second-generation" (*Writing Machines* p. 27).

## Part 10. 2012, *How We Think*

N. Katherine Hayles, *How We Think: Digital Media and Contemporary Technogenesis*. Chicago and London: University of Chicago Press, 2012. ISBN 978-0-226-32140-0 (cloth), 978-0-226-32142-4 (paper). Copyright 2012 by The University of Chicago (copyright page PDF index 4, text layer; render made). Author line: "professor of literature at Duke University."

`K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\How We Think_ Digital Media and Contempora - N. Katherine Hayles.pdf`

**Read 30 Oct 2026 in full**: chapters 1 to 8 and the three interludes (pp. 1-248), the chapter 8 "Coda: Machine Reading *Only Revolutions*" (with Allen Beye Riddell, pp. 242-247) and the notes (pp. 249-253). Works Cited scanned for her self-citations; index not read. 296 PDF pages, 106,737 words in the file; 90,495 words on pp. 1-248 (PDF indices 15-262, notes excluded).

**Folio rule for this volume.** Printed page = PDF index minus 14 throughout the body (index 15 is p. 1, index 262 is p. 248). Native publisher PDF (Adobe InDesign CS2, February 2012): the text layer is the typeset text; no OCR slips. Renders of pp. 10, 55 and 91 were made as a check and agree with the text layer.

---

### 10.1 What the book does, in her own frame

"This book explores the proposition that we think through, with, and alongside media" (p. 1). Two frames are announced: Comparative Media Studies, "approaches that can locate digital work within print traditions, and print traditions within digital media" (p. 7), and technogenesis, "the idea that humans and technics have coevolved together" (p. 10, render read). The three reading modes "close, hyper-, and machine" (pp. 11-12, ch. 3) carry the "thread of attention/distraction that runs throughout the book" (p. 12). Chapter 1 is the overview; chapter 2 the interview study of the Digital Humanities (twenty US scholars, CCH at King's and LCC at Georgia Tech); chapter 3 reading and attention; chapter 4 Simondon, Clark, the adaptive unconscious and Tomasula's *TOC*; chapter 5 telegraph code books; chapters 6 to 8 narrative and database (Massey and spatial history; Hall's *The Raw Shark Texts*; Danielewski's *Only Revolutions*). The closing sentence: "In my view the humanities, far from being in crisis, have never seemed so vital" (p. 247).

### 10.2 Technogenesis, now her own word

> "Another way is through the concept of technogenesis, the idea that humans and technics have coevolved together. The proposition that humans coevolved with the development and transport of tools is not considered especially controversial among paleoanthropologists. ... Walking on two legs freed the hands, and the resulting facility with tools bestowed such strong adaptive advantage that the development of bipedalism was further accelerated, in a recursive upward spiral that Andy Clark (2008) calls 'continuous reciprocal causation.'" (p. 10, render read)

Two modifications make it "contemporary technogenesis": the Baldwin effect and epigenetic change (pp. 10-11, 100), so that "evolution can now happen much faster" (p. 10). The word that was Hansen's in 2008 (two uses, both reporting him) is now the subtitle and appears 47 times; Hansen is cited for *Bodies in Code* on embodiment's flexibility (p. 98) but not for the term. "Contemporary technogenesis, like evolution in general, is not about progress" (p. 81); "as a phrase, it does not specify the direction or human value of the changes, whether for good or ill" (p. 18). The relativistic clock figure for measuring change from inside it (pp. 81-82). The book's own status: "the book as itself a technogenetic intervention" (p. 17); the coda "concludes with an instance of technogenesis redefining the codex as a digital technology" (p. 247).

### 10.3 Attention, materiality and physicality

> "To develop this idea, I distinguish between materiality and physicality. The physical attributes of any object are essentially infinite ... Materiality comes into existence, I argue, when attention fuses with physicality to identify and isolate some particular attribute (or attributes) of interest. Materiality is unlike physicality in being an emergent property. It cannot be specified in advance, as though it existed ontologically as a discrete entity. Requiring acts of human attentive focus on physical properties, materiality is a human-technical hybrid." (p. 91, render read)

The same claim in chapter 1: "attention comes into play as a focusing action that codetermines what we call materiality" (p. 14). Kirschenbaum's forensic and formal materiality are read as examples (pp. 91-92). *My Mother Was a Computer* is cited for the infinity of physical attributes (p. 91). Attention is "the limiting scarce resource" (p. 14; Fred Brooks, pp. 11-12) and "an essential component of technical change (although undertheorized in Simondon's account)" (p. 103).

### 10.4 Hyper and deep attention; close, hyper and machine reading

"Hyper reading correlates, I suggest, with hyper attention, a cognitive mode that has a low threshold for boredom, alternates flexibly between different information streams, and prefers a high level of stimulation. Close reading, by contrast, correlates with deep attention" (p. 12). Chapter 3 restates the 2007 argument (pp. 69, 99, citing "Hayles 2007a"), adds Sosnoski's hyper reading (p. 61), Carr, Dehaene, Nielsen's F pattern, Small's fMRI study and her own critique of fMRI inference (pp. 66-68), Moretti's distant reading, Lenoir's "Forget meaning ... Follow the datastreams" (p. 33), NELL (pp. 70-71), and the pottery series as the definition of pattern: "a pattern consists of regularities that appear through a series of related differences and similarities" (p. 74). "saying computers cannot read is from my point of view merely species chauvinism" (p. 72). The proposed disciplinary sentence: "Literary studies teaches literacies across a range of media forms, including print and digital, and focuses on interpretation and analysis of patterns, meaning, and context through close, hyper, and machine reading practices" (pp. 78-79). Stiegler's critique of her hyper attention answered in n. 3 (pp. 250-251). Guillory cites her (p. 58). "hyper attention" 19, "deep attention" 8, "attention" 79.

### 10.5 Nonconscious, unconscious, the technological unconscious

Chapter 3 "begins to develop a theory of embodied cognition encompassing conscious, unconscious, and nonconscious processes" (p. 55, render read). "nonconscious" 17, all in chapters 3 and 4 (pp. 55, 87, 92, 94-96, 101-102, 104, 106); "unconscious" 51. The sources are the "new unconscious" or "adaptive unconscious" (Bargh, Wilson, Dijksterhuis: pp. 14, 94-97), Thrift's "technological unconscious" (p. 96, now kept under his name rather than renamed as in 2008), Damasio and Sacks (p. 92), Dennett's multiple drafts (p. 105), Malabou's plasticity against flexibility (pp. 82-83, 101-102). Her own body in the argument: throwing pots, "embodied cognitions of many kinds participate, including unconscious and nonconscious ones running throughout my body and, through the rhythmic kicking of my foot, extending into the wheel" (p. 92). "The position taken throughout this book is that all cognition is embodied" (p. 17); objects with sensors "have an umwelt" (p. 17, n. 4 Uexkull and Cog). The pair "cognitive nonconscious" is not yet formed; "technological nonconscious" of 2008 does not recur.

### 10.6 Embodied, embedded and extended cognition

Clark's EXTENDED against BRAINBOUND (pp. 93-94), Hutchins's navy vessel (pp. 92-93), Feynman's "I actually did the work on the paper" (p. 93); "human agency becomes less a 'controller' in Clark's model of extended cognition ... and more of a player among many 'influential forces'" (p. 94). "Embodiment then takes the form of extended cognition, in which human agency and thought are enmeshed within larger networks" (p. 3). The 1999 body/embodiment pair is not restated; "embodiment" 5 only, "embod*" 55.

### 10.7 Simondon, technical objects and complex temporalities

Technical elements, individuals, ensembles (p. 87); concretization and technicity (pp. 88-89); Mackenzie's metastabilities (p. 89); Latour's folding of time (p. 89) and "a tool is 'the extension of social skills to non-humans'" (p. 88); skeuomorphs as "the past is enfolded into the present" with her Honda Accord dashboard (p. 89); Stiegler's tertiary retention (p. 90). Objects "understood as constantly changing assemblages" (p. 13). The time scales table of neuron, sensation, word, narrative (p. 104). "Beta versions are now often final versions" as "the technical equivalent to hyper attention" (p. 105). *TOC* as "a technotext (Hayles 2002)" (p. 106).

### 10.8 Narrative and database as natural symbionts

Against Manovich's "natural enemies" and Folsom's viral metaphor: "Rather than being natural enemies, narrative and database are more appropriately seen as natural symbionts" (p. 176; "Ah, the power of metaphors", p. 175); "The dance (or as I prefer to call it, the complex ecology) of narrative and database" (p. 176); "narrative is an ancient linguistic technology almost as old as the human species" (p. 179); "humans, who can arguably be defined as meaning-seeking animals" (p. 180); memes and Blackmore's temes (pp. 188-189); Massey's "lively" space against absolute space (pp. 183-192); object-oriented against relational databases (pp. 192-196). Liu's separation of content from form in *RST* (pp. 200-204); the Ludovician as "a feedback loop between signifier and signified" against Lacan's sliding (p. 216); the double ending as Rorschach (p. 218). *OR*'s constraints, the octet, the anticoncordance (pp. 224-237); "as if the words actually on the page operated like electrons. ... Such quantum effects, if I may call them that, are everywhere manifested in *OR*'s linguistic strategies" (p. 238, the book's only "quantum"); the chi-square against the Brown corpus finding *in*, *was*, *were*, *been*, *as*, *like*, *or* absent (p. 246).

### 10.9 Telegraph code books

Chapter 5 is the archival case: "the first globally pervasive binary signaling system" (p. 123); "economy," "secrecy," "simplicity" (p. 124); sound receiving and neural change (pp. 127-128); "Mother was dead but now recovered" (p. 129); code books as "an inscription technology enrolling human subjects into technocratic regimes" (p. 130); Saussure distributed across code word and phrase (pp. 132-133); "Telegraphy had its own version of Derridean differance" (p. 135); Otis: "metaphors do not 'express' scientists' ideas; they are the ideas" (quoted p. 147); the body/telegraph analogy and du Bois-Reymond (pp. 147-148); Gilmore on "O Susanna" and the riverboat *Telegraph* reading (pp. 149-151); Wiener on telegraphing a human being (p. 151); Weaver's "Tower of Anti-Babel" (pp. 161-162); Hallner's *Scientific Dial Primer* (pp. 163-169). "As a former chemist, I am particularly charmed by an early version that used electrolytic reactions" (p. 127). Gessler's collection and the code book site with Riddell (p. 158, n. 1 p. 251).

### 10.10 The other spine terms, counted

Denominator: 90,495 words, PDF indices 15-262 (pp. 1-248), notes excluded.

### 10.11 Her self-citations

In the body: "Hayles 2007a" (hyper and deep attention, pp. 69, 99); "Hayles 2001" (*Patchwork Girl*, p. 78); "Hayles 2009" (RFID, p. 96); "Hayles 2002" (technotext, p. 106; media translation, p. 202); "Hayles 2005" (*In the Cage*, "The Dream of Information", p. 130); "Hayles 1999" (the Macy conferences and "the man in the middle", p. 131); *My Mother Was a Computer* named (p. 91). Works Cited also lists "Constrained Constructivism" (1993), "Translating Media" (2003) and "Narrative and Database: Natural Symbionts" (*PMLA* 2007b), the last the source of chapter 6's title argument. The 1984 and 1990 books: 0.

### 10.12 Found in passing

1. **"Technogenesis" passes from Hansen's word to hers** between 2008 (two reported uses) and 2012 (subtitle, 47 uses); she credits Clark's "continuous reciprocal causation" and Baldwin, not Hansen, for its content (p. 10).
2. **"Intermediation" disappears** (0 uses) after 54 in 2008; "Comparative Media Studies" and "technogenesis" take its place as the names for the program.
3. **The noun "affect" is used in her own voice for the first time** in the run (p. 106, twice; section heading p. 238), still in the sense of response to a text, not Spinoza's or Massumi's.
4. **Materiality redefined** as "when attention fuses with physicality" (p. 91), an "emergent property" and "a human-technical hybrid"; the 2002 definition ("the interplay between a text's physical characteristics and its signifying strategies") is not quoted.
5. **"Natural symbionts"** (p. 176) is the one new coinage set against another critic's metaphor; the book's quarrel with Manovich runs through chapters 6 to 8.
6. **Physics returns only as figure**: the relativistic spaceship (p. 81) and "quantum effects, if I may call them that" (p. 238, n. 5 citing Siegfried 2010 for "a summary of quantum effects as they are currently understood"). No Bohr, no complementarity in the physics sense.
7. **Her autobiography**: "a small town in North Carolina" (p. 2); "my computer goes down ... I feel as if my hands have been amputated" (p. 2); "a background in scientific programming" (p. 19); "As a former chemist" (p. 127); throwing pots (pp. 74, 92); Phi Beta Kappa Scholar visits (p. 69); the Honda Accord (p. 89); "I don't know if you have had a brain scan, but I have" (pp. 66-67); Borges changing her mental landscape (p. 208).
8. **Haraway 1988 and Harding 1986** stand in Works Cited with no body use found by whole-word count; either an orphaned entry or a use in a figure caption or note not scanned.
9. **A misprint in her own name for Clark's concept**: "Mark James Baldwin" (p. 100) for James Mark Baldwin (p. 10, correct).

## Part 11. 2013, *Comparative Textual Media* (edited, with Jessica Pressman; Introduction and section forewords only)

N. Katherine Hayles and Jessica Pressman, eds., *Comparative Textual Media: Transforming the Humanities in the Postprint Era*. Electronic Mediations 42. Minneapolis and London: University of Minnesota Press, 2013. ISBN 978-0-8166-8003-0 (cloth), 978-0-8166-8004-7 (paper). Copyright 2013 by the Regents of the University of Minnesota (copyright page PDF index 4, text layer).

`K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\Comparative Textual Media_ Transforming th - N. Katherine Hayles.pdf`

**Read 30 Oct 2026**: the Introduction, "Making, Critique: A Media Framework," by Hayles and Pressman (pp. vii-xxxiii, PDF indices 7-33), and the three unsigned section forewords (Part I "Theories," pp. 1-3; Part II "Practices," pp. 97-99; Part III "Recursions," pp. 199-200), which the Introduction says are the editors' ("the forewords for each of the three sections", p. xxxii). The twelve contributors' chapters were not read; their uptake of her terms below comes from a term scan only. 369 PDF pages, 121,722 words in the file; the Introduction is 9,790 words including its reference list (indices 7-33).

**Voice rule for this volume.** Unlike the 1991 and 2004 introductions, this one is **co-written**. Every sentence quoted from it is Hayles and Pressman's jointly; three passages are marked "one of us (Hayles)" (pp. xvii, xx, xxi, xxx) and are the only words the text assigns to her alone. Spine cells for 2013 say "Introduction, with Pressman" and no cell is read as hers singly.

**Folio rule for this volume.** A calibre conversion (creator string "calibre (1.29.0)", no producer), so the page images are the publisher's but the PDF is an ebook reflow: the text layer is clean and the running heads carry the roman folios. Introduction: printed roman page = PDF index read as a numeral (index 7 is vii, index 33 is xxxiii). Body: printed page = PDF index minus 34. No render was needed for the Introduction; p. xii was rendered and agrees with the text layer.

---

### 11.1 What the Introduction does

"The approach we advocate is comparative and media focused. It recognizes that print is itself a medium" (p. vii); "comparative textual media (CTM)" named as the volume's version of "comparative media studies" (p. vii). The argument: comparison denaturalizes (Radhakrishnan's autorickshaw, p. viii); "writing surfaces have always been complex" (Cayley, Bolter, p. ix); "recursive feedback loops between form and content are not only characteristic of special cases (which we have elsewhere called technotexts [Hayles 2002]) but are the necessary ground from which inquiry proceeds" (p. x); the "Other" of comparative media studies as other media, as media begetting media (McLuhan, Kittler, Siegert, Bolter and Grusin), as "nonhuman objects" in speculative realism and OOO (Harman, Bogost, Meillassoux, Bryant), with Latour's "flat ontology" and "technical and human change as coevolutionary processes" (Stiegler, Hansen, "N. Katherine Hayles (2012)") (pp. xi-xii); "a media framework" as the umbrella term (p. xiii); Latour's "run out of steam" and "an ethic of making" against "the culture of critique" (pp. xiv-xv, xix); Humanities Labs, Bruzelius's cathedral course, Pressman and Brantley's Yale seminar (pp. xv-xviii); Weimar and MIT site visits (pp. xx-xxii); the chapter summaries (pp. xxiii-xxxi).

### 11.2 The sentences that touch the spine

> "Yet another focus seeks to understand technical and human change as coevolutionary processes that were determinative in the physical, psychological, and biological development of Homo sapiens; scholars working in this vein include Bernard Stiegler (1998), Mark Hansen (2006), and N. Katherine Hayles (2012). Human cognition in this view is deeply bound up with technics on conscious, unconscious, and nonconscious levels, with media technologies serving to support, scaffold, and extend cognition in ways consequential for social, economic, and political structures." (p. xii, render read)

> "Drawing on Andrew Pickering's (1995) idea of the 'mangle of practice,' we can say that concept, object, and practice coevolve and codetermine one another" (p. xxviii).

> "Hence our introduction's title, 'Making, Critique': read across the comma, it can be understood as the status quo of endorsing critique, but acknowledging the disruptive punctuation introduces another element into the picture: 'making.'" (p. xix)

The 2012 book is cited in the third person for the coevolution thesis; "technogenesis" itself appears only in that title (p. xxxii). "Nonconscious" once (p. xii), the first appearance of the word in an edited volume's front matter. The speculative-realist and object-oriented names are listed as a strand of "media-focused inquiry" and set beside Latour's actor network theory (pp. xi-xii); Barad, Bennett and "new materialism" are not named, so the 2005 phrase "New Materialism" does not recur here and the 2017 meeting with the new materialisms has not yet happened. "Decentering of the liberal humanist subject" (p. xxxi), "a posthuman computer game" (p. xxiii), "a postmodern, posthuman construction" (p. xxvii) carry the 1999 vocabulary into the summaries. Women's needlework as textual media (Trettian, p. xii) is the one gendered note.

### 11.3 The section forewords

Part I (pp. 1-3): "new media is an unfortunate term" (p. 1); the laptop as "the dying instantiation" of BRAINBOUND AI, cognition "as a whole-and-extra-body faculty" (p. 1); "the movement of human cognizers through intelligent environments" (p. 3); Thrift's "technological unconscious" and Clark's EXTENDED, so that "new kinds of assemblages come into play" (p. 3). Part II (pp. 97-99): Latour and Woolgar's "practice revolution", tacit knowledge, Pickering's mangle as "a spiral twisting upward" (pp. 97-99); "the comparative impulse in CTM is both expressed by and implemented through the practice-theory dialectic" (p. 99). Part III (pp. 199-200): "Recursion can be understood as folding a work's logic back on itself"; Escher, Hofstadter's *Godel, Escher, Bach* ("recursions hint at a lack of absolute foundations"), the Fibonacci base case; "a recursive loop is created in which the medium references the content and the content references the medium" (pp. 199-200). The forewords are the one place in the volume where "cognizers" and "assemblages" appear in the editors' voice.

### 11.6 Found in passing

1. **A co-written introduction**: the 1991 and 2004 introductions were hers alone; this one is Hayles and Pressman's, with "one of us (Hayles)" four times. The edited-volume rule in this file ("her voice is the introduction only") is narrower here: her voice is unseparated from Pressman's.
2. **OOO and speculative realism named, Barad not**: the 2013 map of "nonhuman" positions runs Harman, Bogost, Meillassoux, Bryant, Latour; the new materialisms chapter of 2017 is not yet in view.
3. **"Making, Critique"** is the first programmatic statement of the "ethic of making" (p. xv), carried from the 2012 Digital Humanities interviews ("We make media", Lenoir) into a curricular manifesto.
4. **The title word "postprint"** is in the subtitle only; it does not appear in the Introduction's text (0 by whole-word count). It becomes the 2021 title.
5. **Hofstadter's *Godel, Escher, Bach* returns** (Part III foreword, p. 199) for the first time since the 1984 and 1990 books, as the source for recursion.
6. **"Cognizers" and "assemblages"** appear in the editors' Part I foreword (p. 3) but not in the Introduction.
