## Part 12. 2017, *Unthought*

N. Katherine Hayles, *Unthought: The Power of the Cognitive Nonconscious*. Chicago and London: University of Chicago Press, 2017. ISBN 978-0-226-44774-2 (cloth), 978-0-226-44788-9 (paper), 978-0-226-44791-9 (e-book); DOI 10.7208/chicago/9780226447919.001.0001. Copyright 2017 by The University of Chicago (copyright page PDF index 4, text layer). Dedication "For my students."

`K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\Unthought_ The Power of the Cognitive Nonc - N. Katherine Hayles.pdf`

**Read 30 Oct 2026 in full**: Prologue, chapters 1 to 8 (pp. 1-216), notes (pp. 217-221); acknowledgments read for prior printings; Works Cited scanned for her self-citations; index not read. 263 PDF pages, 96,049 words in the file; 79,739 words on pp. 1-216 after the running heads were stripped (PDF indices 13-228).

**Folio rule for this volume.** Printed page = PDF index minus 12 throughout the body (index 13 is p. 1, index 228 is p. 216). Native publisher PDF (InDesign CC 2015); the text layer is the typeset text, with one artefact: the small-capital running heads are extracted in mixed case ("The CogniTive nonConsCious and The CosTs of ConsCiousness") and the hyphenation leaves a space after each hyphen ("non- conscious", "re- enactments"), silently closed inside quotations here. **The running heads were stripped before counting**: a first pass counted "cognitive nonconscious" 106 and "cognitive assemblage" 197 because the Part 1 and Part 2 heads carry those phrases on every even page; the figures below are from the stripped pass (231 head lines removed). Renders of pp. 22, 30, 68 and 116 were made and agree with the text layer.

**Prior printings** (acknowledgments, pp. x-xi): most of chapter 2 as "The Cognitive Nonconscious: Enlarging the Mind of the Humanities," *Critical Inquiry* 42.4 (2016): 783-808; most of chapter 5 as "Cognitive Assemblages: Technical Agency and Human Interactions," *Critical Inquiry* 43.1 (2016); portions of chapter 6 in *Parole, ecriture, code* (Les presses du reel, 2015); portions of chapter 8 as "Cognition Everywhere: The Rise of the Cognitive Nonconscious and the Costs of Consciousness," *New Literary History* 45.2 (2014): 199-220. The acknowledgments give the *Critical Inquiry* volumes as "4 (4)" and "5 (1)"; Works Cited corrects the first to 42 (4).

---

### 12.1 What the book does, in her own frame

"Unthought" is Le Guin's Handdarata word for "a kind of thinking without thinking" and names "the terra incognita that beckons beyond our received notions of how consciousness operates" (p. 1). The project: "to analyze and explore the nonconscious cognitive assemblages through which these distributed cognitive systems work" (p. 2); "to propose a definition for cognition that applies to technical systems as well as biological life-forms" and "excludes material processes such as tsunamis, glaciers, sandstorms" (p. 3); "to formulate the idea of a planetary cognitive ecology that includes both human and technical actors" (pp. 3-4). The four takeaways: "most human cognition happens outside of consciousness/unconsciousness; cognition extends through the entire biological spectrum, including animals and plants; technical devices cognize, and in doing so profoundly influence human complex systems; we live in an era when the planetary cognitive ecology is undergoing rapid transformation" (p. 5). Part 1 (chapters 1-4): the framework, the neuroscience, the new materialisms, two novels. Part 2 (chapters 5-8): cognitive assemblages in traffic control, digital assistants, somatic surveillance and drones; high-frequency trading; *The Intuitionist*; the humanities. The last sentence: "It all depends on what kind of world you want" (p. 216).

### 12.2 Cognition defined; cognizers and noncognizers; actors and agents

> "Cognition is a process that interprets information within contexts that connect it with meaning." (p. 22, italic in the original, render read)

Its genesis in Shannon and the Fredkin sentence: "The meaning of information is given by the processes that interpret it" (p. 23, cited as "Hayles 2012, 150"; see 12.12 below); Simondon's processual information (pp. 23-24); the definition parsed clause by clause (pp. 25-27): "interpretation implies a choice", "Choice here, of course, does not imply 'free will'" (p. 25), "context includes embodiment" (p. 26), "humans do not have a lock on which contexts and levels are able to generate meanings" (p. 26). "technical systems can never be fully alive. But they can be fully cognitive" (p. 22). The tripartite pyramid of modes of awareness, nonconscious cognition and material processes (pp. 27-29, fig. 1 p. 40), "perhaps better described as a dynamic heterarchy rather than a linear hierarchy" (p. 29). Cognition bestows "flexibility, adaptability, and evolvability" (pp. 29-30).

> "Recognizing that binaries can facilitate analysis (their limitations notwithstanding), I propose another distinction to replace human/nonhuman: cognizers versus noncognizers." (p. 30, render read)

The human/nonhuman binary "inadvertently reinstalls human privilege in the vastly disproportionate weight it gives to humans" (p. 30, naming Bennett 2010, Grosz 2011, Braidotti 2013). "I suggest reserving the term actors for cognizers, and agents for material forces and objects" (p. 32); "punctuated agency" (p. 32); "The better formulation, in my view, is not a binary at all but interpenetration" (p. 32). CHOICE-II (interpretation of information) against CHOICE-FW (free will) (pp. 35-39). Thinking distinguished from cognition (p. 14); cognitive biology (Kovac, Lyon and Opie, pp. 14-16); plant cognition and the Brenner controversy (pp. 16-20); Maturana and Varela's informational closure as the obstacle to technical cognition (pp. 20-21); "The one motivation that all life-forms share is the struggle to survive" (p. 31).

### 12.3 The cognitive nonconscious

"Although the cognitive capacity that exists beyond consciousness goes by various names, I call it nonconscious cognition" (p. 9). The definite article: "In choosing the definite article (the cognitive nonconscious), I intend not to reify these systems but rather to indicate their systemic effects" (p. 2); "nonconscious cognitive processes" for individuals (p. 2). The "new" unconscious "(in my view, not an especially felicitous phrase)" grouped with consciousness as "modes of awareness" (p. 10); the psychoanalytic unconscious as its traumatised subset (p. 27). The functions: somatic markers, sensory synthesis, speed, pattern, inference, and "to keep consciousness, with its slow uptake and limited processing ability, from being overwhelmed" (p. 10). Chapter 2: Metzinger's self-models, "the costs of consciousness" (coherence, belatedness, anthropocentrism, pp. 41-45), Damasio's proto-self "corresponds to what I have been calling the cognitive nonconscious" (p. 46), Edelman's reentry (pp. 46-47), Barsalou's simulation (pp. 48-49), Lewicki's $100 experiment (pp. 50-51), Dehaene's "ignition of the global workspace" and the half-second (pp. 53-55), "acting much more like Joe Biden than Dick Cheney" (p. 55), the McDowell-Dreyfus debate (pp. 56-60), mindfulness and *The Embodied Mind* (pp. 60-63), speculative realism (p. 63). "nonconscious" 276; "cognitive nonconscious" about 50 in running text after the heads are removed; "consciousness" 276; "unconscious" 38.

### 12.4 Cognitive assemblages

> "The term I use to describe these complex interactions between human and nonhuman cognizers and their abilities to enlist material forces is 'cognitive assemblage.' While Latour and Deleuze and Guattari (1987) also invoke 'assemblage,' a cognitive assemblage has distinctive properties not present in how they use the term. In particular, a cognitive assemblage emphasizes the flow of information through a system and the choices and decisions that create, modify, and interpret the flow." (pp. 115-116, render read)

Against Latour's shouted order (p. 115); Latour's mediators and power kept (pp. 116-117), his charge against critical sociology turned on "the critique of power as it was displayed in the humanities during the 1970s, 1980s, and beyond" (p. 117); Deleuze and Guattari's agencement "privilege desire, affect, and transversal energies over cognition" (p. 117); "Why choose assemblages rather than networks ... Assemblages, by contrast, allow for contiguity in a fleshly sense, touching, incorporating, repelling, mutating" (p. 118); "I prefer 'assemblage' over 'network' because the configurations in which systems operate are always in transition" (p. 2). The sites: ATSAC in Los Angeles (pp. 120-123, site visit November 2014), VIV (pp. 124-126), the sociometer and "somatic surveillance" (pp. 126-131), the MeMachine (pp. 129-131), drones and the laws of war (pp. 131-139), Brandon Bryant's "moral injury" (p. 140); "when we design, implement, and extend technical cognitive systems, we are partially designing ourselves" (p. 141). Chapter 6: the future anterior and derivatives (pp. 143-147), Mauer's "theological unconscious" (p. 148), MacKenzie's three phases and the volatility smile (pp. 149-150), her grandmother's string (p. 150), LTCM (pp. 150-152), the Flash Crash (pp. 159-162), "vampiric capitalism" (pp. 159, 171-172), "a weapon in the nonconscious cognitive arms race" (p. 165), IEX and batch auctions (pp. 165-169), Stiegler's print-centric tertiary retention (pp. 170-171), Hansen's "atmospheric media" and Whitehead (pp. 172-174), "Critical Studies in Finance Capital" (p. 176). "cognitive assemblage" about 99, "assemblage" 148 after the heads are removed.

### 12.5 The new materialisms, Barad and Bohr

Chapter 3 is the run's first direct engagement. Their shared propositions (decentering, lively matter, ontology "often along Deleuzian lines", continuum, transformation) and "After the baroque intricacies of the linguistic turn, these approaches arrive like bursts of oxygen to a fatigued brain" (p. 65). The charge: "Conspicuously absent from their considerations are consciousness and cognition" (p. 65), "a performative contradiction: only beings with higher consciousness can read and understand these arguments" (p. 66); "To reason so confuses decentering the human with its total erasure" (p. 66); nonconscious cognition as "a countervailing narrative to the Deleuzian concepts and vocabularies pervasive in the new materialisms" (p. 67). The argument proceeds by their concepts: ontology, evolution, survival, force, transformation (p. 67).

> "An outlier among new materialists, Karen Barad derives her brand of materialism from the physics-philosophy of quantum mechanicist Niels Bohr. Faced with experimental evidence for the wave-particle duality in the 1920s, Bohr developed an interpretation distinctly different from that of Werner Heisenberg. ... Bohr understood this situation as implying, not that the experimenter has 'disturbed' the measurement, but rather that the position and momentum do not have determinate values until they are measured. As Barad points out, later experiments confirmed his intuition. For Bohr, this phenomenon remained in the realm of epistemology. ... Making the leap into ontology, Barad's strong contribution extends Bohr's insight." (p. 68, render read)

"Bohr" 6, all on p. 68; "Heisenberg" there; "complementar*" 1 and not physics (Damasio and Edelman "have complementary research projects", p. 45); the word complementarity is not used for Bohr. The course "Science Fiction, Science Fact" co-taught with particle physicist Mark Kruse at Duke, who "thought Barad's vision was both reasonable and consistent with empirical results of his field" (p. 69); n. 2 on the de Broglie wavelength of a tennis ball (pp. 218-219). The objection: "Even if the fundamental level of reality is intraactional, does that necessarily imply that cultures are? Here is where the framework of nonconscious cognition can contribute significantly, for the issue of levels is crucial to it" (p. 69); "filling in some of the connective tissue that Barad's argument assumes must exist but that she does not explicitly discuss" (pp. 69-70). The particle/wave duality as analogy for entity/event, "from Barad's perspective we are asking the wrong question" (p. 75); "intraaction" adopted thereafter ("interaction/intra-action", pp. 84, 205). Grosz on Darwin and Deleuze (pp. 76-77); Parisi's *Abstract Sex* as "a paper tiger" on neo-Darwinism (pp. 71-73); Parikka's bees and "the denial of representation" (pp. 74-75); "force" as "a kind of agentless agent" (p. 80), the four fundamental forces, BZ reaction, avalanche (pp. 80-82); Bennett's distributed agency and the Iraq diet speculation (pp. 83-84). The closing sentence: "This is not exactly new materialism, vibrant materiality, imperceptibility, or nomadic subjectivity, but rather a paradigm that, cognizant of scientific and technical knowledges, nevertheless strives to bring about a transformation of traditional views of the place of the human in the world" (p. 85). "new materialis*" about 30; "New Materialism" as her own name (2005) does not recur; the phrase is now theirs.

### 12.6 Braidotti

"Braidotti" 16 (pp. 30, 65, 76-79, 83, 203-204). Named with Bennett and Grosz for the human/nonhuman binary (p. 30); among the ontologists (p. 65); with Grosz as the theorists who "strike some kind of balance between forces/intensities and subjects/organisms" (p. 76); "The Ethics of Becoming Imperceptible" and the "sustainable subject" quoted at length (pp. 77-78): "a folding-in of external influences and a simultaneous unfolding-out of affects", "cracking, but holding it, still"; *The Posthuman* (2013): "I am very independent in relation to them" (p. 78); the objection: "Even as the gazelle feels the lioness's claws in her back, she still desperately kicks to get away. In this sense, Braidotti reinstalls human privilege in the face of death" (p. 79); "surely only cognition, specifically, the higher consciousness that humans possess, can achieve the rapprochement with death that she recommends" (p. 79); "nonconscious cognition offers another interpretive option, a site in which subjects can emerge without implying they are immune to flows and intensities (Grosz's concern), and without requiring that the living human subject should balance at the threshold of disintegration without exceeding it (Braidotti's emphasis)" (p. 79). In chapter 8 she is an exemplar: "For Rosi Braidotti in her quest for an affirmative posthumanism, it was finding the balance point between flux and stability" (pp. 203-204). Braidotti is also thanked in the acknowledgments (p. x). "nomadic subjectivity" once, p. 85 (above).

### 12.7 Affect

The run's first sustained use of the noun: "affect" 19, "affects" 19, "affective" 16, all forms 74 (denominator 79,739). Chapter 7's title "Politico-Historico Affects"; "this chapter focuses on affective forces within assemblages, which as we will see go beyond human responses to the postulated responses of technical artifacts" (p. 178); Berlant's "history, affect, and their interactions" (p. 195), affect as "the body's active presence in the intensities of the present" (quoted p. 195), Raymond Williams's "structures of feeling" (p. 195), "Berlant's essay is a wonderful analysis, so good it makes your teeth ache to read it" (p. 196); "Recall that nonconscious cognition is intimately related to affect, serving as a site of mediation between bodily and visceral actions and the higher modes of consciousness/unconsciousness" (p. 197); "Novels show how affects work in individual and collective human lives" (p. 198). Elsewhere the word is Deleuze's ("affects, intensities, assemblages, and lines of flight", p. 70), Braidotti's (p. 78), Parisi and Goodman's "affective capitalism" (pp. 173, 191, 193), Hansen's "affective states" (p. 172); "emotional and affective 'tags'" (Barsalou, p. 49). The 1984 pairing of affect with mutual influence is now "interpenetration" (pp. 11, 32, 83, 169, 175) and "intraaction".

### 12.8 The two novels and the Whiteheads

Chapter 4: *Remainder*'s narrator as consciousness without nonconscious cognition, "the imperialism of higher consciousness" (p. 90), "My undoing: matter" (quoted p. 88), the "ghost kink" as "the presence of an absence" (p. 96); *Blindsight*'s scramblers who "know but do not know that they know" (p. 102), "a vast arid Turing machine full of self-replicating machinery forever unaware of its own existence" (quoted p. 107), "human cognition can no longer be regarded as the 'normal' standard" (p. 110). Chapter 7: *The Intuitionist*, Lila Mae's genies as nonconscious cognition fed forward (p. 180), "Construct an elevator from the elevator's point of view" (quoted p. 181), the catastrophic failure read as the halting problem "in 'elevatorese'" (pp. 185-187), Chaitin's Omega and "randomness is everywhere" (p. 188), Parisi's "experimental axiomatics" (pp. 190-192), "the two Whiteheads" (p. 192), Saldivar's "speculative realism" as a literary term beside Meillassoux's (pp. 193-194), the seven things novels do (pp. 198-200). "Whitehead" 31: Colson in chapter 7 and Alfred North at pp. 172-173, 192, 203.

### 12.9 The humanities' two paths

Chapter 8: control as "increasingly obsolete, if not outright dangerous" (p. 202) and Wiener's "chaotic mix of hope and dread"; "inflection points" (p. 203, n. 1 p. 221); "It all depends on the kind of humanities you want" (p. 205, the Chicago interlocutor); interpretation and description "recursively embedded in one another" (p. 209, Marcus); "there are no 'all-purpose' algorithms" against Fish (p. 210); Goldsmith's uncreative writing as "Consciousness wearing a (distorted) mask of the cognitive nonconscious" (p. 212); Marshall's "Novels by Aliens" (p. 212); the two paths and "an epistemic break" (pp. 213-214); "humans and technical systems are engaged in complex symbiotic relationships" (p. 216). Guillory's "immeasurable" gap answered again (pp. 208, 214, as in 2012).

In the body: "Hayles 2012" for Fredkin (p. 23, "150"), nonconscious cognition "described in detail elsewhere" (p. 27), neural change (pp. 30, 125), technogenesis and the Pleistocene (p. 83), hyper and deep attention (p. 170), *Only Revolutions* with Riddell (p. 205); "Hayles 2014a" (p. 66); "Hayles 2014b" on Harman and Bogost (p. 63); "Hayles 1999" is not cited in the body, though HWBP is defended by name in n. 6 (pp. 217-218). ### 12.12 Found in passing

1. **Bohr enters her text for the first time in the run as physics** (p. 68), and only through Barad; she keeps his position epistemological ("For Bohr, this phenomenon remained in the realm of epistemology") and credits Barad with "the leap into ontology". Complementarity is not named; the apparatus argument is given instead.
2. **"New Materialism" (2005, her own name) has become "the new materialisms" (theirs)**: the 2005 sentence is not recalled; the chapter ends by saying what her paradigm "is not exactly" (p. 85), including "nomadic subjectivity".
3. **Braidotti is read as reinstalling human privilege** (p. 79) and praised as a model intervener (pp. 203-204) in the same book.
4. **"cultural matrix" returns once** (p. 33), as Wall's phrase; the 1984 "cultural matrix" is not recalled.
5. **The Fredkin citation**: the sentence "The meaning of information is given by the processes that interpret it" is cited to "Hayles 2012, 150" (p. 23); the 2012 reading in this file found it in neither the telegraph chapter nor elsewhere by that wording, and the 2008 book carries it at p. 52 ("the meaning of information is given by the process that interprets it"). A slip in page or book; for the chase list.
6. **"Technogenesis" disappears** (0 after 47 in 2012); "intermediation" stays at 0; "coevolution" and "continuous reciprocal causality" (Clark) carry the idea (pp. 21, 47, 96, 136, 155).
7. **Autobiography**: Duke Kunshan fall 2015 (p. x); the course with Mark Kruse (pp. 68-69); the ATSAC visit (p. 121); "Having lived in Los Angeles for two decades" (p. 122); the Delft conversation (p. 130); the grandmother widowed in 1929 saving string (p. 150); the Chicago Society of Fellows exchange (p. 205); mindfulness at Cambridge, March 2015 (n. 1, p. 218).
8. **Wolfe answered** (n. 6, pp. 217-218): "Wolfe and I agree that human being is grounded in embodiment"; the difference is how wide "posthuman" should be.
9. **A slip in the acknowledgments**: *Critical Inquiry* "4 (4)" and "5 (1)" for 42.4 and 43.1.
10. **Keller and Haraway 0**, as in every book since 1999; Latour 27, the most in the run.

## Part 13. 2021, *Postprint*

N. Katherine Hayles, *Postprint: Books and Becoming Computational*. The Wellek Library Lectures. New York: Columbia University Press, 2021. ISBN 978-0-231-19824-0 (hardcover), 978-0-231-19825-7 (paperback), 978-0-231-55255-4 (e-book); LCCN 2020022410. Copyright 2021 Columbia University Press; the CIP description reads "[2020]" (copyright page PDF index 4, text layer). The series page: "The following lectures were given in May 2016" at the University of California, Irvine (PDF index 2).

`K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\Postprint_ Books and Becoming Computationa - N. Katherine Hayles.pdf`

**Read 30 Oct 2026 in full**: chapters 1 to 5 and the Epilogue (pp. 1-190), notes (pp. 191-209); acknowledgments read for prior printings; bibliography not read; index not read. 246 PDF pages, 75,513 words in the file; 58,158 words on pp. 1-190 after the running heads were stripped (PDF indices 15-204).

**Folio rule for this volume.** Printed page = PDF index minus 14 throughout (index 15 is p. 1, index 204 is p. 190). Native publisher PDF (InDesign 14, Distiller 9.5, December 2020). Two artefacts: (1) the text layer splits the "ff", "fi" and "fl" ligatures with a space ("aff ective", "fi rst", "fl ow") and prints "Th e" for "The", so whole-word counts for words containing them were made on a normalised pass and are marked so below; (2) the book's ten black "X-ray" pages (pp. 16, 79, 97, 136, 155, 173 and others) carry code in a symbol font that extracts as garbage, with a caption that extracts cleanly. Renders of pp. 6 and 13 were made and agree with the text layer.

**Prior printing** (acknowledgments, pp. x-xi): a version of chapter 2 as "Human and Machine Cultures of Reading: A Cognitive Assemblage Approach," *PMLA* 133.5 (October 2018). The book "originated as a series of three Rene Wellek lectures" (p. xi).

---

### 13.1 What the book does, in her own frame

"The term I propose to designate this new state of affairs is postprint" (p. 2): "print to refer to the long reign of bound books produced by printing presses from the fifteenth century to the mid-twentieth century and postprint for books produced after roughly 2000" (p. 2), the half century between being "when computational technologies transformed the printing industry" (p. 2). The catalyst "for all of postprint's diverse aspects" is "the emergence of cognition in artificial media" (p. 6). Chapter 1 sets the framework against book history (Borsuk, Stewart, Hungerford, Striphas, Starre), media archeology (Ernst) and cultural techniques (Siegert); chapter 2 is five nodal machines (Paige Compositor, Lumitype, C/A/T, DocuTech, e-readers); chapter 3 interviews at five university presses; chapter 4 two novels (*The Silent History*, *The Word Exchange*); chapter 5 Borsuk and Bouse's *Between Page and Screen* and Dermisache's asemic books; the Epilogue a photograph of glue marks. The closing line of chapter 1: "Print is dead; long live postprint!" (p. 39); of chapter 5: "The postprint carrier of the information partakes of the information about postprint that it carries" (p. 188).

### 13.2 Cognition restated, and the definition's second wording

> "To emphasize that cognition includes consciousness but is not limited to it, I wanted to position it so it would include affective responses, internal and external sensory systems, nonconscious cognition, and other embodied, enactive, and embedded bodily capacities. I also wanted it to connect with contexts that include internal and external milieu. After considerable deliberation, here is what I came up with: 'Cognition is a process of interpreting information in contexts that connect it with meaning.'" (p. 6, render read)

The 2017 wording was "a process that interprets information within contexts that connect it with meaning" (*Unthought* p. 22); the 2021 self-quotation alters it. Biosemiotics supplies the meaning side (Hoffmeyer, Deacon, Wheeler, p. 7; Uexkull's *Umwelten*, p. 7); "Can Computers Create Meanings? A Cyber/Bio/Semiotic Perspective," *Critical Inquiry* 46.1 (2019) is cited (n. 12, p. 192). "cyber-bio-evolution, where evolution through generational change is measured in months" (p. 8); "the two great inversions" of mandate (survive and reproduce / design and purpose) and of trajectory (uncertainty to abstraction / abstraction to uncertainty) (pp. 10-11); "flexibility, adaptability, and evolvability" (pp. 8, 11); "cybersymbiosis" and the work in progress "Cyber-bio-evolution: A General Ecology of Cognitive Assemblages" (p. 14), the seed of the 2025 book.

### 13.3 Cognitive assemblages, against Latour and against the Deleuzian hyphen

> "In appropriating the term assemblage, I draw on the work of both Bruno Latour and Gilles Deleuze and Felix Guattari, with some differences. In Latour's invocation of assemblage, often used as a synonym for network, objects with no cognitive capacities, such as a hammer or pulley, are agents just as much as cognitive entities are ... In contrast, my view of assemblage makes a clear distinction between cognitive entities and noncognitive ones. In Deleuze and Guattari's texts, 'assemblage' is often how the term agencement is translated. ... In contrast to their usage, I assume that entities may be relatively stable and preexist the assemblage in which they participate." (p. 13, render read)

"Another inspiration for me is Deleuze and Guattari's notion of 'becoming'" (p. 13); Simondon's transindividuation and Whitehead's "processural philosophy" (p. 14); "My hesitation about adopting such philosophical positions wholesale has to do with what I see in Deleuze and Guattari as their neglect, indeed, almost complete erasure, of the mechanisms that work toward preserving the stability of entities" (p. 14); "These reservations are expressed typographically in my subtitle by the absence of the Deleuzian hyphen in 'becoming computational'" (p. 14). The phrase "like Janus, faces in two directions at once" (p. 12). The starter motor as a cognitive assemblage (pp. 8-9); "daily life in developed societies can proceed in normal fashion only because of the incorporation of cognitive assemblages" (p. 12). "cognitive assemblage" 59.

### 13.4 Media archeology and cultural techniques

Ernst's media archeology, "considering events from a nonhuman point of view" (p. 28), its limitation "a tendency to create an either/or binary between human and machine viewpoints" (p. 30); Kittler's *Discourse Networks* as "a shock to thought" in 1992 (p. 31); Siegert's cultural techniques, culture that "bubbles up" from the use of objects (p. 32); the cognitive-assemblage approach "travels along the same path as media archeology in affirming that objects may have viewpoints" and shares with cultural techniques "an intense interest in understanding the chains of connection" (pp. 32-33). The tie back to her own earlier terms: media-specific analysis (p. 5), *Writing Machines* "my artist's book collaboration with designer Anne Burdick" (p. 5; n. 7 credits Burdick as designer, "not a coauthor"), "technotext" against Starre's "metamedia" and his charge of "technological bias" (p. 27).

### 13.5 The machines

Twain: "a machine cannot be made to think, and the thing that sets movable type must think or retire defeated" (quoted p. 46); "In my book Unthought and in this project, I try to avoid the word think because it is loaded with anthropocentric implications" (p. 49); "Paige sought to give the machine too much cognition at a time when cognition could only be implemented electromechanically" (pp. 52-53); the Mark Twain Museum visit, 25 August 2016 (p. 50). The Lumitype's inventors "had in this machine the basic ingredients of a digital computer" (quoted p. 57); she bought Gaul's *The Wonderful World of Insects* (1953), the first phototypeset book (p. 66). The C/A/T's coherent fiber-optic bundle, "spaghetti in the middle" (p. 61), in Gessler's collection. The DocuTech and Chip Holt (pp. 69-73); "a redistribution of cognition within a cognitive assemblage may not necessarily be a zero-sum game" (p. 72). E-readers: "books begin to function as cognizers, capable of knowing where they are" (p. 35); "an ontological rupture from the print era" (p. 77); ownership to access (pp. 80-83); Tenen's "selective asemiosis" and "laminate text" (pp. 83-85, 135). Spiekermann's value-based design measured against the hype cycle (pp. 73-76).

### 13.6 The presses

Interviews at Columbia, Chicago, Duke, California (Luminos) and Minnesota (Manifold), 2017 (notes, pp. 200-202); her ties disclosed in n. 5 (p. 200), including Electronic Mediations codirectorship and "Distinguished Research Professor at the University of California, Los Angeles". Crewe's monograph arithmetic (p. 94); Thomas: "The codex is a better technology" (p. 104), self-publishing "is not publishing" (p. 105); Blobaum: "You market a book; you distribute formats" (p. 107); Cohn: "We don't publish monographs, and when we do, it's a failure" (p. 110); Armato's Manifold (pp. 121-124). The metaphors of digital scholarship: "contagion, extension, mutation; remixing, recombining, upgrading, disseminating" against "crystalline prose" (p. 119); nodal against flow models of a career (pp. 119-120); "Cognitive technologies as coauthors and collaborators" (p. 129); "the networked book of the new millennium is not just a cognitive support but a cognizer in its own right" (p. 130).

### 13.7 The novels and the asemic

*The Silent History*: "cognitive contagion" (ch. 4 title), the app's 120 episodes against the codex's 97 (p. 142), the implanted silents as "the very figure of the wandering silent" (Hungerford, quoted pp. 24, 141), "the human essence can survive without language" (p. 151); *The Word Exchange*: "destructive creation" (p. 160), Stiegler's "programming industries" (pp. 161-162, 167), "What would it take to reverse our present plunge into postprint and return us to a regime of print? The answer, apparently, is the end of civilization as we know it" (p. 164). Hansen's feed-forward and "algorithmic governmentality and affective semiocapitalism" (p. 168). Chapter 5: Borsuk's "materialist poetics, citing my book Writing Machines as one of her inspirations" (p. 174); "to exist in the world, language must be embodied, and its embodiments matter" (p. 174); Flash's end-of-2020 death (p. 178); asemic writing "is not writing, but it also is not not writing" (p. 179); Dermisache: "It has to be inside a book, to be read" (quoted pp. 180, 190); GPT-2 tried at talktotransformer (n. 24, pp. 208-209: "This is not true").

### 13.10 Found in passing

1. **The definition of cognition is self-quoted in a second wording** ("a process of interpreting information in contexts", p. 6) against 2017's "a process that interprets information within contexts" (p. 22); the 2025 book quotes the 2017 wording with page (p. 9) and the 2021 wording (p. 94) in turn.
2. **"Cybersymbiosis"** (p. 14) and the announced "Cyber-bio-evolution: A General Ecology of Cognitive Assemblages" are the 2025 book under an earlier name.
3. **The Deleuzian hyphen refused** in "becoming computational" (p. 14): her one typographic statement of distance from Deleuze and Guattari.
4. **Affect survives only as an adjective** (pp. 6, 168); the 2017 chapter is not carried forward.
5. **A GPT-2 experiment** in a note (pp. 208-209), the first appearance of a language model in the run; the 2025 book is built on GPT-3 and GPT-4.
6. **Autobiography**: the IBM machine "fed by IBM cards and large enough to fill a room with all four kilobytes of memory" (p. x; the 2008 book said "4K"); "I live a significant portion of my everyday life through and amid print books" (pp. 17-18); the Mark Twain Museum (p. 50); the rare-book purchase (p. 66); "someone like me, who makes frequent typing mistakes" (n. 16, p. 197); her Duke Editorial Board term (p. 109); Uppsala 2018 and the glue-mark photographs "framed in my study" (pp. 189-190); the Toyota Camry is in the 2025 book, not here.
7. **The *How We Think* subtitle reversed** in two notes (pp. 203, "Contemporary Technogenesis and Digital Media").
8. **Coronavirus** enters in two notes (nn. 35, 55, p. 205), written "before the novel coronavirus pandemic" and marked as more true after.
9. **Bohr, Barad, Braidotti, Haraway, Keller: all 0.** The physics-and-feminism thread of 2017 is absent from this book and returns in 2025.

## Part 14. 2025, *Bacteria to AI*

N. Katherine Hayles, *Bacteria to AI: Human Futures with Our Nonhuman Symbionts*. Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 2025. ISBN 978-0-226-83598-3 (cloth), 978-0-226-83747-5 (paper), 978-0-226-83746-8 (e-book). DOI 10.7208/chicago/9780226837468.001.0001. Copyright 2025 by The University of Chicago (copyright page, PDF index 4, text layer).

`K:\OneDrive\UNM\Dissertation\todo October 26\Bacteria to AI_ Human Futures with our Nonhuman Symbionts - N. Katherine Hayles.pdf`

**Read 30 Oct 2026 in full**: chapters 1 to 10 (pp. 1-248), acknowledgments (pp. 249-251), notes (pp. 253-263); bibliography searched for her own entries and for the interlocutors named below; index not read. Pages 8, 17, 21 and 23 rendered and read against the text layer; they agree.

**Folio rule for this volume.** Printed page = PDF index minus 6 throughout (index 7 is p. 1, index 254 is p. 248). Native publisher PDF; the running heads are letter-spaced small capitals ("c h a p t e r on e", "an integrated cognitive framework") and were stripped before counting (336 head and folio lines). The text layer carries soft hyphens inside hyphenated words ("self-­reference"), removed before counting. One term-scan pass printed page labels twelve too high; every page below was checked against a second pass and the renders.

**Prior printings** (acknowledgments, p. 251; chapter notes): chapter 2 from "Can Computers Create Meanings? A Cyber/Bio/Semiotic Perspective," *Critical Inquiry* 46.1 (2019): 32-55 (ch. 2 n. 1, "first published as Hayles 2019"); part of chapter 4 from "Microbiomimesis: Bacteria, Our Cognitive Collaborators," *Critical Inquiry* 47.4 (2021): 777-87 (ch. 4 n. 1); chapter 6 from "Inside the Mind of an AI: Materiality and the Crisis of Representation," *New Literary History* 53.4/54.1 (2022/2023): 635-66 (ch. 6 n. 1); chapter 8 from "Subversion of the Human Aura: A Crisis of Representation," *American Literature* 95.2 (June 2023): 255-79 (ch. 8 n. 1). The 2021 book's announced title "Cyber-bio-evolution: A General Ecology of Cognitive Assemblages" and its word "cybersymbiosis" do not appear (0 each); chapter 2's subtitle has become "A Technosymbiotic Perspective" (p. 40).

---

### 14.1 What the book does, in her own frame

"This book aspires to combat anthropocentrism by urging a transition from the liberal philosophy we inherited from the Enlightenment to a different conceptual orientation" (p. 2), "the integrated cognitive framework (ICF)" (p. 2), whose three strands are "micro/evo/techno relationality" (p. 2): microbial, evolutionary and technical origin stories, "designed to simulate the braiding process" (p. 17, render read). "The name I use to denote this condition, which is at least as inadequate as most names, is ecological relationality" (p. 19); "Ecological relationality is the (mis)leading phrase that gestures toward the complexities of this project" (p. 20). The second-order emergences of the three strands interacting (pp. 20-21, render read p. 21: gene editing, quantum computing, neuromorphic chips, AIs "achieving human-competitive and more-than-human results"). Chapter summaries pp. 39-49. Chapters: 1 the framework; 2 intermediation with Deacon's teleodynamics; 3 technosymbiosis against autopoiesis and Gaia (Margulis, Lovelock, Maturana and Varela, Clarke); 4 CRISPR, xenobots, "micromimesis"; 5 mineral evolution against biological evolution (Longo, Montévil and Kauffman; Hazen); 6 GPT-3 and the crisis of representation; 7 GPT-4, creativity and regulation; 8 three conscious-robot novels; 9 collective intelligence against Harari; 10 ecological relationality against political liberalism (Lowe, Moore, Robinson). The closing sentence: "the novel itself functions as a giant linking mechanism operating across multiple scales to join the present of us, its readers, to futures in which we may want to live" (p. 247).

### 14.2 Barad and Bohr, engaged directly for the first time

> "I draw on Barad's pioneering work in quantum mechanics to analyze and explain how microphenomena at atomic scale produce the phenomena revealed in quantum experiments. ... Barad argues these must be understood as constitutively coproduced by the choice of apparatus and measurement for a given experiment in a theory she calls agential realism. For Barad, a crucial source of inspiration is the epistemological argument of Niels Bohr, which she extends to ontological claims. Reality as it manifests to us does not preexist the experiments; rather, it is brought into existence as observable phenomena precisely through the process of measurement. Therefore, the specificities of the experimental apparatus are crucial.
> A countervailing force to this argument is the issue of scale. The coconstitution for which Barad argues occurs at the quantum level, but these effects disappear for macrophenomena, primarily because quantum fluctuations are ..." (p. 17, render read)

"Micro Relationalities: Karen Barad" (pp. 21-25): "In contrast to Heisenberg's well-known interpretation of the experiments showing that light could manifest as either a particle or a wave, which posited that the apparatus was interfering with the result, Bohr took a more radical approach, arguing that reality enters the realm of knowledge only once reality is measured. Barad's project takes Bohr's ideas from epistemology into ontology, arguing that quantum reality has no determinate qualities until it is measured with a specific apparatus (Barad 2007, 31)" (p. 21, render read). Representationalism and performativity, "Knowing does not come from standing at a distance" (p. 22, Barad 2007, 49); reflexivity "suspect because it assumes that there is a representation that can be subverted" (p. 22). Diffraction: Barad's diffractive methodology "relies on analogy rather than literal fact" and "sits uneasily with her other claim that she is not dealing in analogies or homologies but literal facts" (p. 24). Note 14: Barad's later work on quantum field theory "does include such case studies, including possible quantum effects in the phylogenetic development of tadpoles" (p. 254); note 15: Barad "apparently unaware of Simondon's work" (p. 254). Simondon preferred for scale: "being as such (the unmediated flux) is discussed by Simondon in terms of potential energy, polyphasal transitions" (p. 25). The word "complementarity" is not used of Bohr; "complementar*" 7, all of frameworks (Deacon's and hers "Viewed as complementary," pp. 40, 51, 55, 57, 58; humans and AIs "complementary strengths and limitations," p. 47; Varela, p. 84). Bohr 4 (pp. 17, 21), Heisenberg 1 (p. 21), Barad 32, "quantum" 27.

### 14.3 Constrained constructivism restated

> "Since it is convenient to have a term to refer to reality as such, I took to calling it the 'unmediated flux.' Crucially, scientific experiments can also give negative answers: 'whatever reality is, this model/perception/theory is *not* consistent with it.' This power of disconfirmation is what to my mind separates science from religion. ... Because of this constraint, I called my own version of the problems addressed in Barad's agential realism 'constrained constructivism' (Levine 1993, 27-43). Yes, our theories are constructed and thus subject to all the vagaries of cultural, political, and economic influences, just as STS work argues, but they are not *simply* constructed, because they are constrained to models consistent with the unmediated flux." (p. 23, render read)

"my modest effort in a similar vein that I called 'constrained constructivism' (Hayles 1993)" (p. 18); Popper's falsification (p. 23, n. 12); Harding's "strong objectivity," "strong precisely *because* it is culturally, politically, economically, and socially constructed. It fits with our evolutionary umwelten" (p. 23); "my scientific training also had instilled in me a rock-solid belief that scientific methodologies are the best options we have to achieve durable, robust, and reliable knowledge about the world" (p. 23); n. 11: "a strong similarity here with Donna Haraway's (1988) notion of 'situated knowledges'" (p. 254). The 1993 essay is in the bibliography with Levine's volume (pp. 27-43). "constructiv*" 6.

### 14.4 Haraway, Margulis, autopoiesis and the observer

"two prominent voices in this effort: Karen Barad and Donna Haraway" (p. 17); "Evo Relationalities: Donna Haraway" (pp. 26-30): "Nothing makes itself; nothing is really autopoietic or self-organizing" (Haraway 2016, 58, quoted pp. 18, 26); sympoiesis, Dempster credited (p. 28); "her hero (and mine) Lynn Margulis" (p. 29); Haraway "in insisting that symbiosis precedes and partially negates autopoiesis" (p. 18); the individual defended against Haraway's "no multicellular organism is an individual" (p. 18); the holobiont objection via McFall-Ngai (p. 29). Luhmann's 1994 Indiana debate with her, "arranged by Cary Wolfe and William Rasch" (p. 28), "Rereading my article now, three decades after it was published" (p. 28; Hayles 1995); "systems theory was setting itself up as the 'other' to narrative" (p. 28). Chapter 3: von Foerster's *Observing Systems* and the observer brought into the picture (p. 35); "What the Frog's Eye Tells the Frog's Brain," assigned by Sperry at Caltech in 1968 (p. 82, n. 6); the "trigger" metaphor that contradicts itself (p. 83); Varela "information does not exist" (p. 84); Margulis's embedded observer: "In the autopoietic framework, everything is observed by an embedded observer; in the mechanical world, the observer is objective and stands apart from the observed" (Margulis 1997, 277, quoted p. 87); "all observers are embedded" as an ICF principle (p. 99); Clarke's "planetary cognition" and Luhmann (p. 79); Fields and Levin's reference frame theory (pp. 94-96); Haraway 1988 again (p. 100). Autopoie* 101, sympoie* 15, symbio* 121, Margulis 89, Maturana and Varela 90, Haraway 25, "observer*" 16.

### 14.6 Cognition, cognizers, actors and agents, carried over

"cognition is a process that interprets information within contexts that connect it with meaning" quoted with page (Hayles 2017, 22) at pp. 8-9 (render read p. 8; the 2017 wording, not 2021's); "I chose 'cognition' and not 'thought' for this framework because, for my purposes here, 'thought' (like one of those inedible fruitcakes soaked in bad whiskey) has an off-putting smell, in this case of anthropocentrism" (p. 8, render read). Actors and agents: lifeforms as actors "always seeking to continue their existences," water and plate tectonics as agents (p. 120; the distinction from p. 10). "cognizer*" 9, "noncognizer" 0. The 1999 book against Harari: "In How We Became Posthuman (Hayles 1999), published sixteen years before Homo Deus, I anticipated many of Harari's observations" (p. 47); "Already in 1999, I suggested in How We Became Posthuman ... that advances in artificial intelligence, robotics, and virtual reality were undermining the basis for the liberal subject of the Enlightenment" (p. 217). The missing half second: "roughly five hundred milliseconds (Libet's famous missing half second)" (pp. 215-216); the narrating self "largely the same as Damasio's autobiographical self" (p. 216). "As I explained in Unthought (Hayles 2017), nonconscious modes of cognition also play essential roles in humans" (p. 202). Intermediation: "a recursive organization that I call a dynamic heterarchy (Hayles 2007b, 100)" (p. 54); lock-in effects "(Hayles 2005)" (p. 40); "intermediation" 22. Hyper attention: "shifting from what I called 'deep attention' to 'hyper attention' (Hayles 2007a). The article attracted Stiegler's notice" (p. 32).

### 14.7 New Materialism, Braidotti, feminism

"My position partially coincides with those taken by many theorists writing under the banner of neomaterialism, including Jane Bennett (2010), Elizabeth Grosz (2011), and Rosi Braidotti (2013), among others. Like them, I agree that material processes have agency" (pp. 10-11); "I can only speculate about why neomaterialists do not choose to move in this direction" of cognition and interpretation (p. 11); "As feminists identified with the New Materialism such as Jane Bennett could have instructed them, physical processes should be recognized as the vital forces they are. Where I depart from Bennett and other New Materialists is in recognizing that the cognitive capacities of living organisms enable them to have a creativity and responsiveness to their environments different in kind from those of physicochemical processes" (p. 125). "As a feminist, I try to avoid masculinist language; for similar reasons, I try to avoid language that reinscribes anthropocentric assumptions" (p. 4). "Feminist theory, along with critical race theory and postcolonial/decolonial theory, are among the areas that have explored relationality at length. Rosi Braidotti, Elizabeth Grosz, Zakiyyah Iman Jackson, and others have interrogated the dynamics of relationality" (p. 17, render read). Braidotti 2 (pp. 10, 17), named and not engaged; Grosz 2; Jackson 2; Bennett 3; Keller 0; "feminis*" 5 (pp. 4, 17, 125); Butler's gender as performance (p. 189); "masculinist" 1.

### 14.8 The machines and the fictions

Fazi's definition of computation (p. 139); Transformer attention as "a homology, which has more constraints than an analogy" (ch. 6 n. 14, answering Raley); Weatherby and Justie's indexical AI and Deacon's "only indexical relationships directly provide information" (p. 145); GPT-3's umwelt, "which I equate (somewhat playfully) with their 'minds'" (p. 152); Dreyfus and Nagel (pp. 151-152); Bender and colleagues' stochastic parrots answered, "parrots, like all lifeforms, also have cognitive capabilities" (p. 156, n. 24 on Alex); Manning and Rees on meaning (pp. 154-155); Felski's recognition (p. 156); four strategies with Branwen's prompts (pp. 156-163); "the ideologies of anthropocentrism" (p. 165). GPT-4 "from correlation to causality" (p. 166); the Microsoft report and the question of a self (pp. 172-173); Slater's "parity between AIs and humans" (p. 175); Traweek's *Beamtimes and Lifetimes* (p. 176); the EU AI Act (pp. 177-178); the Berggruen "Multispecies Lexicon" and rights for AIs (ch. 7 n. 4). Newitz, Ishiguro, McEwan (pp. 180-203): "the liberal humanist assumption that conscious human subjects own themselves" (p. 183); Macpherson's possessive individualism and Locke (p. 184); Klara's boxes as "metaphors rather than as accurate representations of machine vision" (p. 190); Turing's "Ask any dog owner" (p. 202). Harari (pp. 207-221): Dennett and Dehaene misread (p. 210), Graziano's i- and m-consciousness (pp. 210-211), dataism "basically tautological as well as narcissistic" (p. 219), "liberalism is failing in the contemporary world because its vision is too small and too anthropocentric" (p. 219). Lowe, Moore and Robinson (pp. 228-247): "the oxymoronic concept of ethical terrorism" (p. 242); "a strain of schizophrenia runs through the text" (p. 243); carbon coins and blockchain (pp. 234-237); "Planetary reversal: what a concept!" and "a new structure of feeling" (p. 228, Williams's phrase unattributed).

### 14.10 Her self-citations

In the body and notes: *Chaos Bound* (p. 123, the double pendulum; 1990 in the bibliography); "Constrained Constructivism" (pp. 18, 23; 1993); "Making the Cut" (p. 28; 1995); *How We Became Posthuman* (pp. 47, 86, 217); *My Mother Was a Computer* (pp. 40, 52); "Unfinished Work: From Cyborg to Cognisphere" (p. 69; 2006); "Hyper and Deep Attention" (p. 32; 2007a); "Intermediation: In Pursuit of a Vision" (p. 54; 2007b); *How We Think*, **dated 2015 twice in the text (p. 32) and in the bibliography**, where it is also placed out of sequence after 2017; *Unthought* (pp. 1, 9, 69, 202, 216); "Literary Texts as Cognitive Assemblages" (p. 66; 2018); "Can Computers Create Meanings?" (p. 150; 2019); "Microbiomimesis" (2021); "Approximating Algorithms" (2022a), "Ethics for Cognitive Assemblages" in the *Palgrave Handbook of Critical Posthumanism* (2022b), "Inside the Mind of an AI" (2022c); "Subversion of the Human Aura" (2023). **Not cited anywhere**: *The Cosmic Web* (1984), *Writing Machines* (2002), *Electronic Literature* (2008), *Comparative Textual Media* (2013), and *Postprint* (2021), the book four years before this one.

### 14.11 Found in passing

1. **Barad and Bohr are engaged at length for the first time in the run** (pp. 17, 21-25), and the engagement is a refusal on grounds of scale, with Simondon preferred; the word "complementarity" is not used. The 1984 Bohr (viewpoints, "suspended in language") is not recalled.
2. **"Constrained constructivism" returns after thirty-two years** (pp. 18, 22-23) as her alternative to agential realism, with Popper and Harding; the 1993 essay is cited by its page span.
3. **The 2005 "New Materialism" is now "the New Materialism such as Jane Bennett"** (p. 125) and "neomaterialism" (pp. 10-11, 14), a position she "partially coincides with" and departs from over cognition.
4. **Braidotti is named twice and not engaged** (pp. 10, 17), after sixteen mentions in 2017; Haraway (25) is the feminist interlocutor, with Margulis "her hero (and mine)."
5. **The affect noun is gone again** (0; 2017's 74 forms), as in 2021.
6. **The 2017 definition of cognition is quoted with page (Hayles 2017, 22)**, not the 2021 rewording, so the 2021 variant stands alone.
7. **"Cybersymbiosis" and "cyber-bio-evolution" (2021) are replaced by "technosymbiosis"** (27), and the 2019 subtitle "Cyber/Bio/Semiotic" by "Technosymbiotic" (p. 40); *Postprint* is not cited.
8. **The observer returns through Margulis**, "an embedded observer" (p. 87), and becomes an ICF principle, "all observers are embedded" (p. 99); von Foerster (p. 35).
9. **"The noise of materiality"** (pp. 122, 140, 164) is the book's materiality term; "material metaphor," "technotext" and "media-specific analysis" do not appear.
10. **Autobiography**: Caltech 1968 with Sperry (ch. 3 n. 6) and "fudge factors" (ch. 6 n. 10); the 1994 Luhmann debate (p. 28); the 2011 SLSA debate with Stiegler (pp. 31-33); the grandmother (p. 48); Gessler's "Rocks from Space" (p. 127) and his collections (p. 251); the car that beeps when she changes lanes (p. 150); "As a teacher and parent" (p. 225); "I, an English professor" (p. 169); honorary fellow of the Berggruen "Future Humans" project 2023-24 (ch. 7 n. 4); UCLA English and Duke Literature (p. 250).
11. **Slips**: *How We Think* dated 2015 (p. 32, bibliography); Wolfe 2024 is an unpublished lecture; Hazen's team said to extend "for ~3.5 Ga to the present" (p. 127).
12. **Liberalism to be revised, not abandoned**: "A better approach in my view, lies not in heralding liberalism's collapse but in rethinking its premises" (p. 219); "my own admittedly utopian schema" (p. 231).

### 14.13 To chase

1. Hayles, "Constrained Constructivism: Locating Scientific Inquiry in the Theater of Representation," in Levine, ed., *Realism and Representation* (1993), 27-43: the source she returns to.
2. Hayles, "Making the Cut: The Interplay of Narrative and System, or What System Theory Can't See," *Cultural Critique* 30 (1995): 71-100, the Luhmann debate.
3. Hayles, "Ethics for Cognitive Assemblages: Who's in Charge Here?" in *The Palgrave Handbook of Critical Posthumanism* (2022b), uncited in the body.
4. Woods, "Prosthetic Symbiosis," *Centennial Review* 22.1 (2022): 157-86; Wolfe, "Embodiment, Enaction, Representation," Berggruen lecture, 14 March 2024.
5. Barad's quantum field theory work on tadpoles (ch. 1 n. 14), given without a citation.

NOTES: as of 30 Oct 2026 thirteen of thirteen volumes have been read; no spine columns say "not yet read" and no absence may be read off them. Each Part states what its file is: the 1984, 1990 and 2005 files are Acrobat ClearScan reconstructions (the 30 Oct inventory called the 1990 file native; its producer string says otherwise); the 1991, 1999 and 2004 files are page-image scans with OCR text layers (the 1999 file's metadata is empty); the 2013 file is a calibre ebook conversion; the 2002, 2008, 2012, 2017, 2021 and 2025 files are native publisher PDFs whose text layers are the typeset text. Quotations from the scanned and converted files were render-checked where a Part says so. The Cornell file is an Acrobat ClearScan reconstruction: its text layer carries character-level slips ("arc" for are, "sec" for see, "afkct" for affect) which are silently corrected inside quotations; a rendered page is a check on the text layer and not a photograph of the print, and for anything that turns on a single character the print copy is the arbiter. Only the fourteen pages listed have been read from a render; every other quotation is checked against the text layer. Zeros are counts of words with the stated denominator, not readings for an idea under another name except where a cell says "nearest"; the footnote split leaves residual footnote text on a few pages, and a ClearScan mis-reading can hide a word (the "afkct" case is one in ten for that word). Population for Appendix A: 234 quoted strings, of which 194 are Hayles quotations (181 check out, 12 differ only by the PDF's OCR, 1 confirmed by direct reading), 39 are Trinh and 1 is a parser artefact. For the 1991 volume only Hayles's Introduction was read; the contributors' pages cited in Part 4 and in the spine come from a term scan and are not a reading of those chapters. The 1999 file is an OCR'd page-image scan, not a ClearScan reconstruction, so its renders are photographs of the print; its text layer carries ordinary OCR slips which are silently corrected inside quotations. The 2002 file is a native PDF whose designed pages set some running text and all quoted samples as images; the pages with gaps are listed in Part 6, and no count from that column is more than a floor. For the 2004 volume only Hayles's Introduction was read; the contributors' pages cited in Part 7 and in the spine come from a term scan and are not a reading of those chapters, and the file's italic font is unmapped so every italic title is missing from its text layer. The 2005 file is a ClearScan reconstruction like the 1984 and 1990 files; its slips ("Zitek", "1930S", "oflanguage") are silently corrected inside quotations. The 2008 file is a native publisher PDF; its one extraction artefact is the "fi" ligature. The 2012 file is a native publisher PDF with a clean text layer. For the 2013 volume the Introduction is co-written with Jessica Pressman and the section forewords are unsigned; no 2013 cell is a reading of Hayles's voice alone, and the contributors' pages cited in Part 11 come from a term scan and are not a reading of those chapters. The 2017 file is a native publisher PDF whose running heads were stripped before counting; its hyphenation leaves a space after each hyphen, closed silently inside quotations. The 2021 file is a native publisher PDF whose text layer splits the ff, fi and fl ligatures with a space (closed silently inside quotations, and closed before counting words that contain them) and whose ten black code pages extract as symbol-font garbage with clean captions. The 2025 file's running heads are letter-spaced small capitals and its text layer carries soft hyphens inside hyphenated words; both were removed before counting, and one term-scan pass that printed page labels twelve too high was corrected against a second pass and the renders before any page number was written. Section headings, the division of spine cells into named, present-unnamed and absent, the "found in passing" list, the proposed citation-log entries and the chase lists are mine, a reading aid and not tags or codes on Amy's material; none of her sentences appears in this file. No em dash appears in this file; dashes inside the book's own sentences are rendered as commas or colons and the page is given so the punctuation can be checked. This file replaces four files created earlier on 30 Oct 2026 (HAYLES_CosmicWeb_deepread_Cornell_2026-10-30.md, HAYLES_concept_spine_1984-2025_2026-10-30.md and .csv, HAYLES_CosmicWeb_quotation_walk_Cornell_2026-10-30.csv), whose content is all here and which were removed at the same time.
